COUNTY PALATINE OF LANCASTER. NOTICE.

PROPOSED NEVi BYE-LAWS FOR THE GOOD RULE AND GOVERNMENT OF THE ADMINISTRATIVE COUNTY. THE MUNICIPAL CORPORATION ACT, 1882, SEC. 23, AND THE LOCAL GOVERNMENT ACT, 1888, SEC, 16. NTOTICE 18 HEREBY GIVEN THAT AT A MEET- ING of the COUN CY COUNCIL of the County Palatine of Lancaster, held in the County Hall, in Pres- ton, in the said County, on Thursday, the Ist day of May instant, the said County Council resolved that, upon the recommendation of the Standing Joint Com- mittee, the said County Council two-thirds of the whole number of the Council being present; doth here- by make the following Bye-Laws undor the provisions ' ot s-c. 16 of" The Local Government Act. 1883," and subject to the approval of the Secretary of State, Viz : INTERPRETATION OF TERMS. Throughout these Bye-Laws the following words and expressions shall have the meanings hereafter re- spectively assigned to them, unless such meanings be repugnant to or inconsistent with the context, or sub- ject matter in connection with which such words or expressions occur, namely :- 1. " Street" inclUdes any highway, public bridge, road, lane, foot-way, squhi%-, court, alley, or passage op en to the public,whether a thoroughfare or not. , , 2. "Public Dace" includes any common, public park or walks, pleastire or recreation ground, road- side waste, churchsard, chapel yard, cemetery, rail- way stat‘on, m•rket. whether established under Charter or otherwise , and any other place, ground or land to which the public have access for the time being. BETTING IN STREETS. .

I.—No yerson shall froquont or use any street or olter public place either on behalf of himself or of any other person ler the purpose of bookmaking or betting or wagering or agreeing to het or wagir with any person. SHOOTING GALLERIES, ROUNDABOUTS. &c. 2.—No person shall keep or manage or use or per- mit to be used a shooting gallery, roundabout, organ, or other like machine on land adjoining or near any street or public place, 60 as to be an annoyance or dis- turbance to any residents in the neighbourhood. Provided always that this Bye-Law shall not apply to any fair or feast lawfully held. STREET CRIES.

3.—No person elven, for the purpose of hawking, selling, or deAributing any article whatsoever, call or shout continuously in any street so as to cause annoy- ance to any residents., or to persons occupying shops, warehouses or offices in the neighbourhood. BEHAVIOUR IN STREETS AND PUBLIC PLACES. 4.—lf two or more persons assembled together wilfully obstruct the free rise of the street, or ailfullv jostle or annoy any foot-passenger, and continue such obstruction, jostling, or annoyance, each such person shall be guilty of an offence, and shall be liable, no conviction, to a penalty hereinafter provided. s.—No person shall wilfully or persistently loitedat or near the entrance of any church, chapel, or other place of public worship, during the time of Divine Ser- yice,or during the assembly thereat or departure there- from of the congregation( or in any churchyard or cemetery during the time of a burial service or cere- mony in such a manner 89 to obstruct or cause annoyance to any person or persons going to, attend- ing at, or returning from any such place of public worship, chureliyard,or ;cemetery. 6.—No person shall, to the annoyance or interrup- tion of any person passing along, residing, or being in any street or public place, make use of any violent abusive, profane, indecent, or obscene language, gesture, or conduct, or commit, solicit, incite, or pro- voke any person to commit any act of indecency, or sing, recite, or read aloud any profane or obscene song, or ballad, or book. 7.—No person shall wilfully deface or obliterate the name or any part of the name painted or placed for the information of the public of any street or public place, or wilfully damage any guide post, mile stone, or mile post, or other publio notice.or wilfully deface, or obliterate any word or figure painted, cut,or other- wise inecribed thereon. B.—Ni. person shall, on any public coach •T other public vehicle for the conveyance of passengers, or on any coach, brake, wagonette, car, or other vehicle hired or wed for the conveyance of passewoers, blow any born or use any other noisy instrument,or make or combine with any other person or persons to make any loud sineine or outcry while in or passing along any street or public place to the annoyance or inter- ruption of any of the residents, provided that nothing in this Byes Law shall prevent the use of a horn in a reasonable manner by one person on & coach or similar vehicle. 9,—No person shall in, upon, or to any building, public lavatory, drinking fountain, or pump, or any wall, fence, embankment, gate, door, pillar, tree, or post in or abutting on any street or public place, post, paste, or affix, or shell employ any person to poet, paste, or affix, or shall in any way causs to be posted, pasted, or affixed any indecent bill, notice, or placard, including any advertisement, relating to the treatment sf clisesse, or shall paint or draw any indecent plc. turn, figure, or sketch, or write any indecent language. 10.—Every person who shall offend against any of the foregoing Bye-Laws shall be liable for every such offence to a fine not exceeding Five Pounde. AND NOTICE lo ALSO HEREBY FURTHER GIVEN that a copy of such Bye-Laws, sealed with the Corpor- ate Seal of the said County Council, has been sent to His Majesty's Principal Secretary of State for the Home Department for allowance by him, at the expiration of 40 days from the date hereof,pursuant to the prreaisione of sec 23 of "The Moniclpal Corporations Act, 1882." AN old beggar woman asked a lady the other day for a halfpenny. "I've nothing for you, said the lady; "but if you go to the soup kitchen you will get a pint of excellent soup." "Soup is it ye mane?" bawled the indignant memifcant ; "do you call that stuff soup? Sure and I'll just tell you how they make it. They get a quart of water, and then boil it down to a pint, to make it strong."

- A CASE OF ABSENT-MINDEDITESS.—Mr. Mont- gomery was making his way across the floor of a small ball-room which he had just entered. Tho room was comfortably filled with scions of old families who were enjoying a private hop. Mr. Montgomery was attracting considerable attention, and he was aware of this fact. The trouble was that he could not account for it. He was a chi hi of a noble race himself, and at no time in his life did his inborn dignity shine more conspicuously than now. He had passed the early part of Um evening with convivial friends, but he did not con- nect this fact in any way with the interest that was being shown in his promenade. Others did, for the truth was that, the night being stormy, Mr. Montgomery had raised his umbrella previous to his entrance and was still holding this useful article over his head, apparently with the idea that its removal would be the ruin of his dress. suit.

The evening gowns made for this season's debutantes are of the softest and flimsiest materials, and are noteworthy by reason of their simple elegance. The fact, however, of their exceeding fragility makes the prettiest evening toilettes an expensive, almost extravagant, item in the outlay incurred in introducing a young girl into Society. The economical mother will, however, lessen the expense by providing her daughter with one or two really good silk or satin underdresses, which can be used again and again with a fresh overdress of lace or net. Indeed, girls who move in less exalted society would find such a plan economical, as, with the aid of a dress stand, they can themselves reno- /vete an old gown and make it as good as new, even if their knowledge of dressmaking is not very [extensive. Common sense and good taste, together with an overdress (with ready - made skirt and material for bodice such as one can buy anywhere) are all that is required, and the task will be considerably lightened if some pretty model gown has been duly and carefully noted, and is imitated as faithfully as possible. It is always a good plan to spend a morning hilt specting the windows of the best dressmaking establishments before selecting a toilette of any nature, as one, if observant, may pick up many little hints as to the tendency of the modes, and so have even a simple gown rendered distin- guished by those little effective touches which one is so apt to overlook, and which go so far to make a successful tout ensemble.

The pretty ball gown sketched is of soft pale blue areophane over a cream silk underdress. The toilette is trimmed with garlands of creamy

EVENI roses shading to pale pink, a little fine lace finishing the décolletage. There are quantities , of flowers of all sorts used this season for even- ing wear, arranged as berthes, chatelaines and sprays, while some of the most charming sleeves are composed entirely of chains of flowers. The rose, especially the small ba.nksia species, is the chief favourite, whether because of its natural beauty, or because of the important position it will bold during the Coronation season, it is difficult to say. A very beautiful ball gown of white esprit net had its edge bordered with forget-me-nots, while loops of ribbon and small knots of the flowers decorated the front of the bodice. One is never wearied of reiterating the charms and fascinations of lace, and it is used on all articles of dress profusely, sometimes different varieties being intermingled. There are many dainty lace skirts now sold in the shops, ready to mount on slips for evening wear, some of which are charmingly embroidered with raised leaves and flowers in silk, while others again are braided with silk. The more inexvensive kinds, of overdresses are of d'esprit net decorated with numerous lines of satin ribbon about an inch wide. Silk gauzes printed with lovely designs of flowers and foliage are much in vogue, while mousseline de soie and chiffon are -employed in the construction of many charming

Flowers are much worn in the coiffure this season, and a pretty sample of this mode is shovvm in my sketch. Very artistic wreatba uf

EVENING COIFFURE. artificial flowers so cunningly fashioned as to almost rival natlire. are sold for the hair, and are particularly becoming to a fresh young face. The fashion for twining chiffon in the hair is also very pretty and set off a certain stamp of face in a wonderfully becoming fashion. It only re- quires clever fingers and a study of the features and head to achieve success. Of course, the more classic the features the better the effect. In the realms of dress one of the most notable features is the revival of the old - fashioned coloured pipings which were beloved by our mothers in their youth. Among other quaint fancies are rouleaux of satin in colours on white toilettes, while satin ribbons are also used in quantities round the bottom of skirts, or as borderings to flounces. Cashmere, especially in white, is a material whose charms I fancy are hardly appreciated, and in cream or ivory white it is particularly attractive. Its draping qualities are admirable and its wear almost endless. The full flowing skirt still holds its own, and will continue to do so throughout all the summer season, but there are many other varieties which have much to recommend them. Among these

SKIRT,

are the skirt with a close-fitting ompiecement round the hips, into which the skirt proper is set. The fulness in such is set into pleats, gaugings, or volants. This is, indeed, quite one of the most chic skirts of the moment, and when, as illustrated, guipure lace covers the empiecement and is continued into the lower part of the bodice, the effect is excellent. This mode will be much employed for cotton or batiste dresses, as these lend themselves kindly to the necessary pleating. In light materials also favour is found for the exploiting of the skirt decorated at the hem with triple flounces, the edges of which are generally finished by narrow ribbon or lace edging. The skirt with the shaped volants, one over the other, is popular for cloth cdstumes. Some venturesome votaries of fashion are having their more elaborate toilettes flounced up to the waist with tiny flounces some four or five inches deep, on the edges of which narrow bibe ribbon is run, These are mostly employed on taffetas or foulavi gowns reserved for fetes or garden parties.

AN OLD SCORE,

ADELINE SERGEANT (Author of " The Lady Charlotte," to.)

===== 0002601_19020523_art0009.txt | included=true | included =====
PART il. —thurrEs 111.

Everybody said that Mrs. Vandeleur was a flirt— so much of a flirt that careful mothers warned their daughters against making friends with her, and snetaphorically drew their skirts aside when she went by. She was an audacious, brazen-faced, hard- hearted little woman—according to the report of these austere matrons—, atrociously pretty and be- wilderingly smart in repartee. She was a widow, and certainly showed no disposition to marry again; indeed it was rumoured that she would lose her money if she did, the late Ifr. Vandeleur having been something of a curmudgeon, advanced in years and given to fits of insane jealousy; but if Mrs. Vandeleur could not marry, she amused herself all the more by flirting with all the men she met. She was over thirty, but did not look it, and she had the dangerous gift of a most extraordinary attractive- ness, of which her beauty formed only a part. There was this to be said in her favour : she kept the friends she made. Men lost their heads about her now and then, but she never lost hers; and when

their period of madness was over, they settled down with a pleasant kind of friendliness which she really liked better than their love-making. Women said that Laura Vandeleur could never bear to see a man escape altogether from her hands. But then some- how or other, women did not like Laura Vandeleur. She looked very pretty as she sat by the fire in her drawing-room one afternoon in November: she had just come back to town, and found her rooms in- tolerable - without fragrant wood fires and great bowls of roses and violets. The weather was grey and cold; nobody lmew that she was in London and she thought herself safe from visitors. It was with some vexation that she heard the servant an- nounce a caller. She was, as always, beautifully dressed ; there was no need for her to wish herself differently attired; but she was reading a novel and she disliktd putting it down. The little pucker on her forehead vanished, however, as the visitor en- tered the room, and was succeeded by the most brilliant of smiles. " Maurice ! Can it be you ?" She held out her little white hands in greeting. "How dear of you Why, I have pot seen you for months !" He pressed her ihands and murmured something inaudible. She was sheared by his appearance. He was pale and had dark marks round his eyes, and she noticed that the veins stood out upon his hands as if he had grown thin. "Have you been ill ?" she asked abruptly. But there was tenderness in her face. "A touch of jungle fever, that was all," Harding said, sitting down listlessly in the chair that she in- dicated—very near her own. "I am subject to it now and then. It always pulls me down for a few days." "It does indeed, if this is how you look after it," said Laura to herself, but she simply rang for tea and began to busy herself with the silver bells of her toy terrier, just for all the world as if she had no interest in Maurice Harding at all. Until she had given him a cup of tea, she scarcely took any obvious notice of him. But all the while she was thinking of him and wondering what ailed the man who, for many years, had been so staunch a friend to her. Only a friend, alas! She did not think that he could ever be anything more. He, meanwhile, luxuriated in the warmth of the room, the comfort of the chair in which he sat. He was not a Sybarite by nature, but he was ill and sad, and felt vaguely soothed and consoled by his surroundings. His eye roved from the flowers at his elbow to the graceful little figure in the arm-chair. The light of the fire just caught the gold of her hair till it shone like a glory around her head: the jewels at her neck and wrists and on her white fingers sparkled with many coloured lights. 'Hard- ing was in some ways an unsophisticated man: it did not occur to him that Mrs. Vandeleur's pose was assumed for effect, or that the soft rose and white of her complexion was lees due to nature than to art. He looked at her so fixedly, so admiringly, that Laura at last turned almost nervous beneath the gaze, and in order to break the silence asked him what he was looking at. , "At you," he answered at once. " I was think- ing how beautiful you were—you will pardon the freedom of an old friend, I hope. And how different —from some women." " Different?" said Laura' with a smile. "You know how to be a true friend : 'you do not pretend and /mislead—what am I saying? You must not mind what I say, Laura; lam not well. This I touch of fever—" "Is it all fever?" she asked gently, bending to- Wards him. "Is there not also some grief—some anxiety—" " You have heard ?" he said suddenly. "I have heard nothing. I only guess. Someone has wronged you—deceived you— 7 • "It was my, own fault," Hardin° answered bit- terly. "I injured her in days gone by, and she has never forgiven me. She refused me—three weeks ago, and gloried in the pain she made me suffer." "Then she must belt brute," said Mrs. Vandeleur. Her hands tightened their grasp on one another, and a light came into her eyes. "You are well rid of her, Maurice. She must be a hard-hearted woman. Can you not contentyourself with your true friends2,ll Harding was silent for a moment or two, then uttered a sort of groan which made Mrs. Vandeleur wince as she heard it. "I loved her," he said, from under the hand with which his eyes were covered. "Are you the first person who has loved in vain?" said Laura, in a steely. voice. "Be a man, Maurice, brace yourself a little; don't spend your strength in mourning for a girl who has thrown you over—es- pecially when it is only for the sake of a revenge." "But she was right. I don't blame her." Mrs. Vandeleur tightened her lips for a moment. Then her whole face relaxed, and she said in a voice of exquisite tenderness: ' "Tell me all about it, you peer boy." The words seemed inaplaropi.iate to the bronzed. face and manly figure of lier visitor, and yet they fell like balm upon Harding's ear. Like every man, he forgot that Laura Vandeleur was a beautiful woman, younger than himself, and not without her own susceptibilities: he heard only the kindlinaSs of her tone and put it down to the innate motherliness of which he believed every woman to be possessed. He yielded his confidence at once. In a few broken sentences, he told thd story of his ill- fated wooing, of Olwen's refusal of his proposal, and her ream for hating him. "You see she had justice I on her side," he ended gloomily, with a heavy sigh. " Ohs flan hardly expect a woman to forgive such an insult, such an injury, as I inflicted upon her." "Perhaps not? said Mrs. Vandeleur softly. "Women feel these things so deeply, you see. Evidently Miss Dare is not a person who forgives very easily. Surely it would he better to put her out of your mind altogether, Maurice other women exist—who like you, whom you have not Injured—" She paused, a little ashamed of herself. For a moment it seemed to her as if she had absolutely proposed to him to marry her. But it had evidently aot atm& Harding in that light. He shook his head despairingly, "I shall never love another woman. I never saw one who touched me in the least until I met Olwen 'Dare."

Laura bit her lip. But recovering herself almost instantly, she spoke with resolute cheerfulness. "There is one point I want to have quite clefs, Maurice. Lionel Borrodaile was your ward, I kno*; but wards are not often so submissive to their guar- dians as he seems to have been." "His father had left me supreme control over hun until he should be twenty-five years old. I could have cut off his income entirely for a time. With some men, the threat of doing this would have been powerless, but Lionel hated exertion or hardship of any kind. After a little futile resistance he gave way—especially when I impressed upon him the bad account I had heard of the girl—not Olwen Dare, as it turned out, but the woman in charge of the school where she was working—" "You were careless about your facts, Maurice." "Yes, and she blames me—who would not ?—for that. I was so anxious at the time, you see, to free him from the toils of an adventuress, as I considered her—that I was ready to use any means that came to hand. I am punished now." "So Lionel escaped. And afterwards he went to Italy. You remember ?" " Yes, I think you once told me he met you there."

did. But I never told you, Maurice, all that I knew of him. Ultimately he died, as you know, from the effects of hard drinking and hard living. He began that downward career in Italy. Does this woman—this Olwen Dare—know the sort of man for whom she still cherishes a tendresse ?"

"I don't suppose she does. / could not tell her, you know."

"NO, I suppose not. But—someone else might." "It would be no use. It is not only that she re- members Lionel, but also that she hates me."

. " Ah !" • The long elawn intonation ended in a sigh. It was inscrutable, and Harding lifted his eyes to dis- cover wt it rueant._: Ue_saw a lovely vision.before

him. The gofiren hair slime 11U-the-aureole of a saint around the sweet, pityin' gfaee, with its delicate colouring, its wonderful expressiveness of feature. He fancied that there was even a tear in the be- witching blue eyes. He had never seen Laura Vandelear so entirely at her best. It was a moment of weakness with him. out his hand impulsively. "Laura," he said. " You are a true friend. I know nobody like you. Won't you take pity on me and be my friend—always? You know how little I have to offer you, yet—there is only you who could in anyway—"

He put

He stopped abruptly. She had risen to her feet, quickly, as if something had stung her, thrilled her with sudden pain. Involuntarily her hand sought her heart. It had given a startling leap, and was now beating so hard and fast that she couldhardly find voide with which to utter what she wished to say. "You don't know—you don't know what you are saying," she faltered at last. "You can't tell all it means—l think I might be able—in time—to make you happy, Maurice—but--" "If you desert me, Laura, I shall have nothing left," said the man sadly and simply. She'stood on the hearthrug looking at him and tearing her handkerchief to rags between her restless hands. After a long pause, she spoke, in a stifled voice.

"1 will answer you in twenty-four hours," sho said. "You must give me that time in which to think. I can say nothing now." Then she pressed her torn handkerchief to her eyes and passed out of the room, leaving Maurice Hard- ing to his own reflections.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

The spring of the Coronation year of Queen Victoria was a most Iselenient one, such as we have this season found so trying to health and temper. Instead of en- joying early summer sunshine, and trying a dain. y on muslin, chiffon, and lace toilettes, we have all been wrap in furs, and raincoats, and sitting over May fires. Many people like to get their spring cleaning over ear:y and knock cif fires, but this year such a procedure hAe meant some weeks of the untold misery of never feeling warm. It was the same in 1333, but the summer af tee- I wards was not a bad one on the whole, and we can only hope that we shall be equally favoured later on this year. The Queen's decision that tiaras are to be worn at the Coronation ceremony,will add much to the gorgeoue- ness of the historic pageant. Despite rumors to the contrary, this particular form of ornamentation h3s been built in high, up-standiog fashion, it being found that this arrangement would prove much more effective in front of a coronet, on the auspicious day. Among noted jewels in evidence at the Coronation I ceremony, will be those of the Duchess of Marlborough, of whose famous pearls and diamonds we have all heard so much. Then there will appear the Countess of Echester's black pearls, whist are not only famous, but absolutely unique. Lady Carnarvon's splendid emeralds, aid Lady Wimtorne's superb rubies. The Marchioness of Londonderry, too, will wear some magnificent jewels, so also will the Countess of Aberdeen, the Countess of Shaftesbury, Countess Cadegan, Georgiana,Countess of Dudley, and Princess Pine. Altogether the blaze of jewels during the progress of the Coronation festivities promises to be quite unprecedented and overwhelming. Not only the Peeresses who are to attend the Corona- tion, but hundreds of other women who hope to he bidden to one or other of this season's Court functions, are keeping the jewellers busy just now. The tendency would appear to be to revive old potterer', and old fashioned stones, and ornaments are so arranged that they may be worn crone, or detached and appear in many forms. The rose is a very favourite design fce brooches, pendants, and hair ornaments, but there i 3 a suggestion of heaviness about it, and moreover it wi I " date" one, later on. Pink topazes, peridets—a beautiful stens in an indescribable !bade of delicate green-blue—and oliveeens, which closely resemble a clear pale emerald, are being extensively utilised for mixing with diamonds, but pretty as the fashion is, it will probably be short lived. The many little concessions granted with regard to the coronation robes, will make it possible for the major- ity of wOMS2 attending to look tolerably well, but it cannot be hoped that they will look their best. Such et toilette is not familiar and cannot be comfortable nor,- over, provided that summer weather has arrived by then, the unfortunate wearers must become exceedingly warm. Then the long hours ! Apart frown the fac- that numbers of these great ladies will have had their hair drersed the day before, a procedure which will render sleep I.or them almost impossible, they must be in theft seats in the Abbey soon after cock-crew. At the conclusion of the ceremony, these weary peeresses. and peers, will proceed to the Palace of Westminster, where they will lunch, and wait for the crowds to disperse enough fee the drive home to be possible. At the three Courts held by the King and Queen,the pallor and weariness of the latter bar been apparent to all. From the eminence of the ereat tower of the new cathedral at Westminster, two crowns of flame will dominate on both nights,and from what is known of the treatment of the embarkment and the gardens, a fairy fete will be created. The spectacle from the Thames promises to b a very beautiful one, all the bridges to Westminster having brilliant inuminetiene. About 2,000 invitations are iesued for a State Ball, and about 1.200 for a State Concert. These entertainments, as also the Courts,are given in the ball-room at Buckisg- horn Palace which is a magnificent apartment, nearls 140 feet in length. Naturally these isle erowns are difficult of manipulation but skill and taste triumph, and loyal subjects have presented to them very excellent rniniatnres of their Majesties' Crowns. We have already been told that it is a mistake to sup- pose that the Garden of Eden was in the neighbourhood of the Tigris And Euphrates, and that it was, in fact, situated in Wales. ....-40.-- A Farm Boy's Singular Death.

Mr F A Jones, deputy coroner, conducted an inquiry in the Coroner's Court, Warrington, on Wednesday morning. respecting the death at the Infirmary of Richard Howard, 13 years of age, residing at Battery Cottages, Woolston. The 11lr8:. witness called was George Howard who stated that the deceased was his grandson and he would have been 14 years of age that day if he had lived. The deceased left home at 630 on the morning of the 10th of May to go to his work as usual at Mr Marshall's farm at Rixtoc. Later on witness heard that his grandson bad met with an accident. Witness accompanied him to the In- firmary at Warrington where the wound was dressed. The deceased was allowed to return home and appeared to go on all right until the following Friday. Next day he was taken to the Infirmary where he died on May 20th.—Edward Mrrshall, farmer at Rixton stated that the deceased had been employed at his farm for the past ten weeks as a farm servant. At nine o'clock on Saturday,the 10th of May witneee in company with the deceased was [ harrowing a field. The deceased was leading the horse and witness was in charge of the harrow. From some reason or other the horse shied and threw tha deceased to the ground and before be could get out cf the way one of the spikes of the harrow caught him on the left buttock andlinflicted a severe flesh wound. The deceased was taken to the lefirmary at Warfington. In reply to the Coroner, witness said he did not know the cause of the horse shying.—Nurse Ilol- royd of the Warrington Infirmary gave evidence et the deceased coming to the institution on the 10th May suffering from a flesh wound. The wound was dressed and be was allowed to go home. When he returned on the following Saturday he was de- tained in the institution. Lock-jaw set in from which he died on Tuesday morning at B.3o.—The jury returned a verdict of" Accidental Death."

CHAPTEB _ IV.

01 won Dare had gone back to a little cottage of her own; a place which she loved because it was so far.from the madding crowd .of London life. It had a charming garden sloping down to the river, and whenever she was tired or sick ,at heart, it seemed natural to her to go there and recruit herself. But the housekeeper wondered a little when she came in November, just when the garden was at its worst yind there was a touch of dampness in all the rooms, which had been so bright and cheerful all the sum- mer. But she said nothing about her surprise to Miss Dare: her mistress had a dignity of her own which did not permit of impertinent questioning. To a visitor, daintily .clad in fur and velvet, a woman of the world who had left London for a day's journey, the place seemed lonely and miserable in the extreme. Mrs. Vandeleur glanced round her, shrugging her shoulders a little, and remarking to herself, with a pretty, cynical air—" She must be in love with him! Else, why should she come to this place of desolation ?" " She rearranged her veil, put up her hand to feel that the golden hair beneath the black toque with its buneh of violets was smooth and neat, then rang the bell at the door. She was admitted and shown into 'a quaintly furnished little drawing- room, " all chintz and china," as she expressed it afterwards, and left alone while the maid went to summon Miss Dare.

Laura Vandelenr was nervous, but she did not show her nervousness. It was with a bewitching smile that she met Olwen, who looked pale and thin, yet beautiful, in a long black dress that seemed to express the tendency of her mind. She bowed politely, but her eyes told the question which she did not put into words—why was her visitor, here? "You will wonder why I have come," said Mrs. Vandeleur, "and indeed 1 hardly know what impulse possessed me to descend on you in this unceremonious manner. My only excuse is this—l am an old friend of a man you know—Maurice Harding." Olwen's face froze at once. She motioned her visitor to a seat, but remained standing as she re- plied. Mr. Harding is an acquaintance of mine, cer- tainly." _ _

" Of course, you know," said Laura, without further preamble, "that he has been very ill"? . _

Olwen sat down. It looked almost as though she could not stand any longer. "I did not know," she said faintly: _ _ _

"It was fever. He was subjeet to it, but Ido not think it returns unless he is suffering from men- tal distress or anxiety. I believe he is suffering very greatly in that way at present. You—perhaps you know why." _

" I have no—no—knowledge of Mr. Harding's affairs," said Olwon, trying hard to retain her self- possession.

"Have you not ?"—Mrs. Vandeleur looked wist- fully at the girl. "Is it nothing to you that he is ill, weak, despondent? You see, we are old friends, be and I. He knew me when my husband was alive—he has been very good to me ; and it was natural that he should tell me his story—forgive me if I speak too frankly—the story of his love for you, and of the rejection which has caused him so much pain." "These things are not generally spoken about," said Olwen with lifted head. "I am surprised* that you should come from him in this manner." "But I did not come from him: I came of my own accord," said Mrs. Vandeleur. And Olwen noticed that her face was very sweet as she said the words. "I came because of my interest in him, and of my desire to see him happy." "1 hope for his happiness too," said Olwen, with averted eyes. "Then—excuse me—you take a strange way ot advancing it," said Laura with evident warmth. "But I must not speak of that. The reason I came to you to-day was that I knew &person—a man— with whom I believe you were once acquainted—Mr. Lionel Borrodaile."

A flush crept into Olwen's pale cheeks. "Mr. Harding has told you a great deal," she Said. " think he has told me everything," said Mrs. Vandeleur quickly. "It would have been useless to tell me half, you see. And.then I bethought myself of Lionel Borrodaile as I knew him, and I felt that I should like to tell you a little about him." Olwen looked full into her visitor's eyes. "You knew him? You liked him? You were a friend of his? But how could you be a friend of his if you are Maurice Harding's friend ?" "Because Maurice Harding was as good a friend to him as he has been to me. You do not know how he cared for that boy in his youth, watched over him, devoted himself to him in every way. The reason why he interfered in a certain attaclunent— • you know what I mean—was because he had received an entirely mistaken idea of the young lady in ques- tion. And Lionel was very easy to convince—in fact, he owned to me a month or two after his return from Devonshire, that he was uncommonly glad ';—that was his expression—' to have got out of the scrape. The girl had bored him long enough.' You must forgive me if I quote his own words." The colour started, hot and crimson, in Olwen's cheeks. She had always indulged in the fancy that Lionel had •sorrowed for her as she for him. It was a shock to find that she had been mistaken.

Mrs. iTandeleur went on softly and relentlessly. "He came to see me when I was in Florence; and—my dear, I must tell you—he made love to me. He was a most ardent lover—for a time. I Awed not recapitulate his vows. Re swore that he had loved no one but me—that his affection for the little school-mistress in the Devonshire village had been all assumed, and that he had never meant to marry her—do you understand ? He threw aspersions on your character which were worse than those which Mr. Harding spoke of in his letter. He boasted of his conquest over you—" "Oh, stop, stop !" cried Olwen, wildly, " I can- not bear this !"

"I would not say it if it were not the truth and entirely for your own good," said Mrs. Vandeleur firmly. "I laughed at the boy, but did my best to be friendly to him for his guardian's sake; but at last I discovered him in a foolish and disgraceful liason with an Italian milliner, and then I was oblige4to give him up. I heard worse tales of him afterwards. Then he began to drink and to gamble, and finally Maurice Harding came out to nurse him in his last illness—galloping consumption brought on by his own excesses. My dear, he was a worth- less, dishonourable scoundrel, from whom you hap- pily escaped; and you should thank Maurice Harding from the bottom of your heart for his caution, instead of trying to punish him for a rats. take whict he has since so bitterly repented of." Olwen was crying, and she did not attempt to hide her tears. "Is he very ill ?" she asked at length, and Mrs. Vandeleur augured well from the question. "Yes, very. Sadly weak, and in want of a woman's tenderness." "It was not that I loved Lionel:" said Miss Dare, rather feebly ; " but I resented his treatment of me and its cause."

" If you did not love Lionel," said Mrs. Vande- leur, with a sudden inspiration, " what hinders you from loving a better man ?" a Nothing," said Olwen. "Only—l thought that I ought to stand upon my dignity." .And she smiled through her tears. "It was an old score—l wanted to pay it ; that was all." "Then you had better come back to London with me," said aura promptly. And Olwen came. • • * * * • • *

But when Maurice Harding called for his answer at Mrs. Vandeleur's pretty little house in Mayfair, he found Olwen waiting for him, and Laura nowhere to be seen. And when Olwen asked him to forgive her for her haraness. what coula he do but take her

"Fri Hi arms and-vow thafEeld E'er tlie bitter Or her valour and her pride ? But, meanwhile, in the dark, upstairs, another imams stool alone, pressing the tears from ant bey ern an ebb told herself that she was glad—glad leat that Maurice should be happy away from her, and that the "old score " had indeed been paid. It had been revenge on Olwen's part: it was gratitude on hers.

SHOCKING FATALITY AT FRODSHAM.

Man Killed by a Threshing Machine.

At the Travellers' Rest Inn, Five Crosses, Frodaham, Mr .1 C Bate, district coroner, held an inquest on Mon— day touching the circumstances attending the death of 'William Turner, labourer, who was run over and killed by a threshing machine en Friday, the 16th inst. Margaret Turner, wife of the deceased, stated that her husband was 25 years of age. He was employed as an attendant upon a threshing machine. He was subject to fits, and had one as late as Wednesday morn- ing. Enoch Ashbrooke, engine-driver, for Mr Hall, Five Crosses, stated that deceased had been in the habit of following his threshing machine. Witness and Mr Hall's son had charge of the engine and machine. Deceased was not employed by Mr Hall. The deceased went with them to Ellesmere Port to Mr Davies's where they did some threshing. They were returning to Kingsley and the accident occurred in Howey-lane, near to Overton Church. Witness was driving the engine, and the deceased was walking alongside the box of the threshing machine. The deceased carried a scotch ready to put under the wheel, but it happened that they did not want it, and he threw it bask into the box, and was walking alongside when witness last saw him. Witness suddenly heard a shout, and, on stopping the engine and getting down, he found the deceased lying between the two straw press wheels. Be was quite dead. His b3dy was under the press, with his bead towards the wheel. Ile was lying on that side of the machine on which be had been walking a few moments before. Deceased was in the habit of following the machine, and he usually scotched the wheels for thern when required. He was not paid for doing this. Wit-. ness could not tell whether the deceased had had a fit, and then fallen down.

The Coroner. The probability is that he would be getting on the shaft to ride ?—Witness : I cannot tell whether that was so.

Had be been working that day P—Witness : No. Had he received any money P—Witness : No. Hs had a trifle to draw which he had earned previously. Had he had any drink ?—Witness : He had had a drink or two on the road.

How much ?—Witness : I cannot say to ha sure. The witness at this point fainted, owing no doubt to the close atmosphere of the mono. We was removed into the open air where he soon retsovered. Several jurors said the accident had, been a shock to the witness.

Harold Greenway, a boy living at Netherton, stated that on Friday evening, about six o'clock, he was going te Frodsham with another boy, and they met the threshing machine in charge of four men. He no tieed the deceased walking by the aide of the machine with a scotch in his hand. He was in front of witness. When witness next observed the deceased be was under the straw press, the wheel having goneover his head. He did net see how he got under the wheel. About four minutes would elapse between the time he saw the de- ceased walking alongside the machine and when he observed him under the press. The man seemed happy enough when witness saw him walking alongside the machine.

Sergeant Hunt stated that from enquiries he had made along the route in Helsby he found that there was no indication that any of the men with the machine were under the influence of drink.

The Corener thought the evidence pointed clearly to the fact that deceased mot his :death accidentally. It was not quite clear how he get under the wheel, but they could only surmise that he had been carrying the ecotch to put under the wheel end, finding it was not required, be had thrown it into the bor. Then no doubs he was returning to get on to some part of the machine to haves ride. If he had been getting on to the front of the hex thto 'driver must have seen him as he was close to. The probability was that he was trying to get on to the connecting abaft, and ia doing he fell, and the wheel of the press want over his head, and he was killed almost immediately.

The jury returned a verdict of ",Accidental death." The deceased's head was terribly crushed by the , wheel.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES. Sankey Bridges Wesleyan Chapel.

Sunday School Anniversary.

===== 0002601_19020523_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
Mr. R. E. Garnett and Religious Instruction. The Sunday School anniversary services in connection with Sankey Bridges Wesleyan Chapel were held on Sun- day, and, as usual, were attended with success. The tanning service was held in tee chapel, the preacher being the Rev J Grange Bennett, who hag occupied the pulpit on many similar occasions. The rev gentle- man preached an appropriate discourse, which was listened to with considerable interest. In the afternoon an enjoyable musical service was held in Parr Hall, and was largely attended. Mr R E Garnett presided. The school children occupied seats at the rear ot the platform, most of the girls being at- tired in white, and the scene was a very pleasing one. The service commenced with the singing of the hymn, " Praise ye the Lord," and afterwards a prayer was offered by the Rev C H Hocken. The scholars then rendered the chorus " Forwaed march with spirit and expression, and it was evident that they bad been care- fully trained for the occasion. Mr DR Dunn conducted ' and Mr F Podmore, M I U M, discharged the duties of organist with ability. The song " Meditation," wag then very sweetly interpreted by Miss B Eckersley, Mr Podnaore accempanying. The rcholars having contri- buted an effective rendition of the chorui " Tribute,' the recitation " Nellie's Prayer," was feelingly given by Miss M H Dunn. The Chairman then delivered an address and, at the outset, congratulated the Sunday school Officials upon having monopolised Mr Bennett's services for theday and also upon their enterprise in securing Parr Hall for the afternoon and evening services. Mr Garnett proceeded to comment upon the excellent singing of the scholars, and said it evidenced careful training by those who understood music. Continuing, the speaker said there were certain matters engaging the attention of the nubile not only of Warrington, but of the country generally, at the present time,which might be termed matters of very serious importance. On these questions different men held different views, and every man had a perfect right le held his own view on any questton which he had tnoronghly considered and theuglet out, but it did not follow that a man having come to a conclusion, had any right to say that that was the only right conclusion, and no one e'se had any right te their thought on the matter. A question which was, perhaps, of vital importance to those interested in Sunday school week, was that of the future education of the children, which appealed to everyone in the couatry. They could look at !the question from one standpoint or another, but gi clog every man full credit for the courage of his con- victions, and feeling bound to honour those convictions, there were certain deductions which could be proved, and which were ant open to diepute. If it were possible for them to gravely eonsider these points, and arrive at a wise decision, he said " let us stand by nur opinions, and net be afraid of them." They had fought for freedom, they had established their rights, they had a right to think for themselves,and a right to educate their children in the way they thought best,and if they had suffered any persecution or trouble, or if they bad made any sacrifices, were they going to give up that position without a struggle and an effort ? He said tbat if it were necessary, further sacrifices should be made on behalf of these who were to follow after them. They ought to be able, and willing and glad to do it. He was not there to dogmatise and to lead the way; he was only there to ask every one of them to go carefully and theroughly into the questions that were exercising the life of the nation at the present time, and when their own decision was arrived at, with the help of these who were able to guide them, they must stand then to that conviction and fight for what they considered to be right. Mr Garnett wend en to express approval of the suggestion of Mr C. E. Parker that religious instruction should be given pietorially— that they should try to teach their children and those committed to their care, not an much by Catechism and words, as by a picture to illustrate the meaning, or by the living example of the man himself. He considered that the suggestion to teach the children by picture and example was better than all the talk and cateehiene in the world. In con- clusion, Mr Garnett said he had not been laying down any dogmatic rules, and he came to the service because he felt he might possibly be received in the place of one who would have been present if he had been able, and whe had helped Sankey Bridges Chapel on previous occasions. The Rev C Ei HOCKEN referred with regret to the absence of Mr Robert Garnett, who, he said, had been Passing through a very serioustillnese. He (Mr Hoeken) was sure they ail felt the deepest sympathy with Mr Garnett, and trusted that he would loon be amongst them again. The children then rang the chorus" Marching along," and Miss B Echerelev delighted the audience with her effective interpretation of " Abide with Me." The chorus " Beautiful home" having been rendered, the service concluded with the well-known hymn " On- ward Chrietiau seldiers." The evening service was held in the Pare Hall and there was again a large attendance. The Rev J Grange Bennett preached a powerful sermon, and another in- ereating musical programme was gone through, including the solo "Sun of my " by Miss Eckersley. The collections for the day realized the handsome sum of £35 163 4d.

The Rev. W. Bracecamp's First

Sermon at St. Paul's, Warrington. On Sunday evening the Rev. W. Bracecamp the newly-appointed Vicar -of St. Paul's Church, Warrington, preached his first setmen as Vicar of the parish. The rev gentleman took for his text the words :—"Christ, the power of God," I. Corin- thians i., 24. Before concluding his sermon, the Vicar said tending in this pulpit fir the first time to-day, I realize that the position which I am now called to occupy is one of much interest and privilege and of great opportunity, but also one of considerable reopen- eibility. In the Providence of God I have been invited by the truste2s of this living to take charge of this church and district. I felt it my duty to accept this offer, believing it to be a distinct call from God to return to the town where I have already lahOured most happily for three years, as also to the diocese in which I was ordained. It is, therefore, with great pleasure that I take up the work of this church and district. In the church 'desire that our worship should be thoroughly devotional and spiritual- Let us seek to carry out the two-fold Apostolic injune. tion to " Let everything he done to edify," and also to "Let everything be done decently and in order." My desire will be to interest myself in everything which I believe will tend to the moral and spiritual welfare of the community, but chiefly my endeavour will be to set before you in due proportion the truth of God's Holy Ward,and so tesch and to preach thallsoule may be saved and believers built up on their most holy faith. My heart's desire is that everyone who worships within these walls may " worship Gcd in spirit and in truth," end that what we say and sing with our lips we may believe in our hearts and practise in our lives. So shall NiTI be able in all humility and yet with all confidence to give an answer to:every man that asketh ulea reason of the hope that is in us, and at the same time prepare our- Selves for the more perfect worship and for the everlast- .ing service of the Holy City in the presence of GAO and of the Lamb. As regards the work in the parish I realize .c'esy seriously that it will be no easy matter to follow such a good man as your late Vicar. H bee left everything in a very flourishing statee Pray that I may have grace and strength given me to lecep up the work ac far as possible. Remember that though in the providence of God the generals or com- manders in Christ's army have been changed the work Is the same. There are still the three great enemies of the Church to contend against, viz., the world, the ail:cfihe, and the devil. I ask then for your kind forbear- , your earnest prayer, your Sthristian sympathy, and at the lame time I would appeal for your personal go-operation. Rally round me as you did round your late Vicar. a -ive me a trial of your love and sympathy and help, remembering that if the work of 3od is to 'roarer in this place it must be by the united action of minister and people. The Christian Endeavour convention.

===== 0002601_19020523_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
Sermon at Warrington.

In connection with the great Christian Endeavour Convention which has been held in Manchester during the past week, a convention sermon was preached in the Emmanuel Church, Bold-street, Warrington on Sun- day afternoon. The preacher for the occasion was the Rev E J Gilchrist, B.D , Presbyterian minister, of Ips- wich- There was a fairly-large congregation composed chiefly of members of the societies affiliated with the Warrington and District Christian Endeavour Union. The devotional part of the service was conducted by the Rev E T Reed, assisted by the Rev J L Milne, 8.D., of St. John's Presbyterian Church. The preacher based an able discourse on the text " But thou, when thou prayest,enter into thy closet,and when thou hest shut thy door, pray to thy Father which is in secret ; and thy Father which Beath in secret shall reward thee openly" Matthew 6c.6 v. There was no such strain upon heart and spirit, he said, as that which was made by Obribtian religious work. That which cut but little to the worker would benefit but little Wm.* whom the worker sought to help. What wonder then that the Christian teacher and worker should at times grow weary. But there was renewal for life's expended powers. There was a place where they could gain more than they had given ; a place where they could receive back again in still greater fulness all they had expended in the service of otbers,for in communion with God there was refreshment for the weary worker and in the consciousness of His love the heart could become still occe more, and the fret and care which came from work and worry were dissipated as they met in the appointed place referred to in the text. What a privilege it was to have a secret place to which they might turn in the hour of need. The preacher proceeded to demonstrate that they could not got rid of sin by trying to cover it up and bury it in the depths of forgetfulness, What was the supreme effort of present day life but to get rid of loneliness. Men did not wish one moment's pauie in which there wee time to think of better things, but filled up every spare moment lest per- chance a man should think. When a man went into the secret chamber and took his sin with him, and he was alone with God, there was time for thought. It was-unmanly for anyone to determine his life heedlessly I without thought. The preacher proceeded to refer to the need at entering the secret plane when , the heart was bowed down with suffering. The ' heart knew its awn bitterness. In the secret place they met with the Father and the Father himself would give them strength to bear the burden if only they would see'l it in the secret place. To teachers and workers in Christian enterprise the preacher advised them to seek inspiration by communism with God in the secret place. That which would tell meet of all in their lives was not the word spoken or the work done, but the spirit of their lives, and that influence would depend upon their likeness to Christ. They would become ' like Him most of all in the secret place.—A collection I was takenon behalf of the Manchester Convention funds. I

I EVENTS OF 'THE WEEK ILLUSTRATED.

To the public, even those intimately in touch with Australian affairs, the news of Lord Hope- toun's resignation came as a very great and de- cidedly unpleasant surprise, as he has been ever since the inauguration of the Federation a most satisfactory and popubir Governor-General. His has been by no means a bed of rases in his position of final court of appeal and go-between amongst the jealous Premiers, each one fighting keenly for his own prestige and that of his particular colony, and his administration has been most markedly successful without his popularity suffering in the slightest, which is a great tribute to his tact. Now as a thunderbolt comes the intelligence that the Federal Parliament has refused his reasonable demand for an additional grant of £B,OOO per annum, pending the establishment of one proper Federal capital, and his consequent resignation. For this he gives the reason that his present salary of £lO,OOO is insufficient to keep up two Government houses and maintain the dignity of his high office,

LORD HOPETOI7N

THE GOVERNOR-GENERAL OF FEDERATED AUSTRALIA WHO

IS RESIGNING.

unless supplemented by large sums from his private pocket. This it is hardly fair to ask from any Governor-General, and there is little doubt that the Colony, through the Federated Parlia- ment, is adopting a very short-sighted policy in its refusal to make an adequate ' grant, especially as Lord Hopetoun only asks for it until such time as only one Government house will be required. This is hardly the way to get men of his stamp and standing to undertake the onerous office, and every Agent-Genoral in London seems to be at a loss to explain the action, as the news has come as much as a surprise to them as to everyone else. Lord Hopetoun will continue to fill his office until after the Coronation and its concurrent festivities, when it is understood that Lord Tennyson will temporarily take over the administration of affairs until a successor has been appointed from home. Meanwhile, Lord Hopetoun will be a decided loss to Australia.

The recent history of Cuba has been stormy in the extreme, and it is hoped that under the new Cuban Republic, formally launched last Tuesday, its unsettled days are destined to give way to a reign of prosperity. First came the revolt from Spanish dominion and then American interference, followed by a temporary administration by the Yankees, but this ceased on May 20th, when the American flag was hauled down and that of a Cuban Republic hoisted in its stead, the reins of government being at the same time handed over to the first President, General Palma. The cruiser Brooklyn was despatched by the American Govern- ment to take part in the festivities and to salute the new flag, all the American troops in Havana being paraded in honour of the occasion, which was naturally marked by great rejoicing among the Cubans themselves after their hard struggls for independence. Then General Wood and his staff vacated their position and sailed for home, leaving Cuba to work out its own salvation as well as it can amidst the constant intrigues which are

he yoqpg King to help to restore his people to their former position amongst the nations of Europe, and now that the festivities have been concluded, considerable anxiety as to the future is being expressed, as the accession of King Alfonso is unquestionably a critical moment in the history of Spain. The programme of rejoicing embraced a reception of the Ambassadors Extraordinary, the giving and receiving of Orders, a great bullfight, a Court Ball, the opening of the Andaulsian Fair in the Bnen Itetiro Park, the opening of the exhibition of paintings of El Greco and Retiro, a battle of flowers followed by a banquet, a tournament and a Moorish fantasia at the Hippodrome, a gala performance of Din Moran= at the Opera, the taking of the oath before the Oortes,,a farewell reception to the Foreign Princes, a great reception to the public function- aries, a monster garden-party in the Royal Park, a reception at the Palace to the students and work- people, and, finally, a re-union in the saloons of the National Library—truly an enormous pro- gramme, which has on the whole been well carried out.

M. Llibet's *visit to Russia must be looked upon as a return to that paid by the Czar to France last autumn, and there is very little reason why any special significance should be attached to this fresh interchange of international amities between these two allies, unless it be that Russia may again be in need of a loan, and in- tending to apply once more to her ever-fruitful source of finance in times of pecuniary need. Great enthusiasm was shown in France over the President's departure, and it is unquestionably a popular move amongst the people in general, who are as Russophil as they are Anglophobe in their tendencies, and in their turn the Russians have given M. Loubet an equally cordial reception. THE CUBAN REPUBLIC AND ITS FIRST

PRESIDENT.

disturbing it internally. However, the selection of their first President is particularly felicitous, and Tomas Estrada Palma is unquestionably the right man in the right place. If anyone can turn the present state of political chaos into one of national order, he can and will, raising the new Republic to a position of prosperity which is well augured for by the generous dealing of the United States in the matter of tariff, A quarter-of- a-century ago General Palma was dragged to Spain in chains and disgrace, and now be returns to rule the island from which he was forcibly exiled, and to fill the executive chair from which he was formerly persecuted and sentenced. Born at Bayama sixty-three years ago, he is a veteran in war, who has always striven for his country's freedom, and during the last war he was minister-plenipotentiary in the States for the insurgent Republic, thereby gaining a thorough knowledge of American civil and political institutions. Such a man at its head bodes well for Cuba, and all lovers of free- dom will wish success to the newly-inaugurated Cuban Republic.

Spain this week has been celebrating the formal accession of King Alfonso XIII. and his taking the Oath before the Coras upon coming of age and receiving the reins of government from the Queen- R.egent, who' has filled the office so well for so many years past. Last week Queen Christina, who is now simply "Queen-Mother," geld her last Cabinet Council and bade farewell to her Ministers, begging them in a very pathetic speech to serve her son as well and faithfully as they had served herself. Senor Sagasta in his turn bade her farewell on behalf of the Cabinet in very eulogibtio terms, dwelling in a very, feeling ity upon the

Last Saturday her Majesty made her last appearance as &gent, at the special request of her advisers, at the ceremony Lefore the Cortes, after which King Alfonso took all affairs of state upon his shoulders. It is generally felt that he has come into his Kingdom at a very trying time, when the outlook of Spain is depressing and unpromising, and the whole country_ with one accord looks to

journey. Those who know M. Loubet's simple tastes will sympathise with the President in the fetes and feasts to which he will ' have to graciously and gracefully submit during his visit to Russia. M. Loubet is happiest when he is dressed in a comfortable suit—a somewhat dusty frock coat it may be and a not aliogether faultless silk hat—ana leading the quiet, unaffected life of an ordinary citizen in France, and particularly in his beloved Montelimar, the place of his birth, where lives his aged mother. There the frock coat is dustier than ever' and the hat almost ruffled. No escort; no etiquette. Emile Lonbet saunters through the cobbled streets, peers into the modest shops. If it rains the President goes about with a voluminous umbrella. If it pours, he seeks shelter in some archway. Recently some curio collector stifile the liasseident's umbrella, but was seen, arrested, Emile Loubet laughed; then asked that the robber should be released, and made him a present of the umbrella. And when his mother meets him at the station he embraces her affectionately; and on summer afternoons mother and son "take air" together. Perhaps he regrets past days when, in a less responsible position, he could more often visit Montaimar. Mme. Loubet cannot be induced to leave her quiet home for Paris; and Mme. Lonbet cried when Emile Loubet had to occupy the anxious, troubled post of President. Nevertheless, it must by no means be imagined that M. Loubet is lacking in true dignity, and it may safely be said that his welcome in Russia will be more genuinely cordial than that accorded to his more pompous predecessor, the late M. Pais Faure, who visited the dominions of the Czar in 1897. Russia itself, meanwhile, is in a very unsettled state, owing to famine amongst the 'peasantry and the riots of the students. In fact, from intelligence which has leaked through de- spite the rigorous censorship at present exercised, things appear to be in a very bad way, and serious trouble is said to be brewing. There seems to be a settled feeling of unrest amongst the Working classes, and the Government is showing its un- easiness by measures of peculiar severity, and the immediate outlook is anything but promising. One bright piece of news for the Russians has re- cently been made public property, and it may have a great political effect. It is announced that the Czarina is expecting an addition to her family of four daughters next September, and the great hope is that this time the Czar and the country will not be disappointed of a sim and heir—a hope that will be echoed throughout Europe.

Again the ever popular Military Tournament. is in full swing and will be until June sth, and its programme is as interesting as ever, despite the demands of the War upon the Army, and the reduced number of troops at present available. SKETCHES AT THE MILITARY TOURNAMENT.

the tournament is being held. One of the chief attractions is a representative group of Indian Officers and Soldiers, who give displays of Indian tent-pegging and exhibitions of skill-at-arms at each performance. This contingent will remain in England over the Coronation festivities, and is quartered at Shorncliffe Camp. During the great final display, always the most interesting feature of the whole show, use is being made of our two recent wars in West Africa, and the public is being initiated into the mysteries of bush-fighting, as practised against the Ashanti i and the Aros by our native troops, officered by whites, while a large detachment of "Blue-jackets" are showing their proverbial handiness' in saving life in case of shipwreck, a skeleton vessel being provided with all necessary search-lights, rockets, and life-saving apparatus. There is also an "Historical Cavalry Bide" in which the numerous changes in the cavalry uniforms during the various periods of British history are illustrated by a large detachment of mounted troops. Displays ,of calalry manoeuvres are also being given,- with a special display and musical ride by the Horse Guards. The Imperial Yeomanry are well represented in the various competitions, and displays are being given by -the Army Gymnastic Staff and the N.C.O.'s under their instruction at the headquarters gymnasium at Aldershot. Altogether the Military• Tournament this year is particularly good, especialty considering the disabilities under which the orgallisera have been labouring.

_ The motion was agreed to. TRH RIIRAL DISTRICT COUNCIL'S ASSOCIATION. THE EDUCATION BILL. The CLERK presented the following statement :—The Rural District Council's Association corresponds to the Poor Law Unions Association. Hitherto tho circum- stance of the Association being iu comparative infancy, and of your Council being represented at each Congress of the Royal Institute of Public Health and at meetings of the Sanitary Institute, Caused consideration of con- nection with the Rural District Counoils Association to stand over That Association is now large and success- ful and is of equal importance to Rural Councils as is the Poor Law Unions Association to Boards of Guardians. The Deputations Order enab!es your Council to appoint two councillors and the clerk as your representatives if the place of meeting of the association is within 50 miles, and one councillor and the clerk if over 50 miles. The Hon Secretary writes A general meeting will he held at the Westminster Palace Hotel en Wednesday, May 28, to be followed by a conference. Sir Edward Strachey, But., M.P., will preside. The matters to be discusred are of considerable moment to Rural District Councils, and it is very desirable that the conference should be as representative as possible. An agenda will be sent ia due course." A motion will be preposei That this Council join the Rural District Councils Association, and that the Council be represented by one member and the clerk at a general meeting of the association, to be held at the Westminster Palace Hotel, London, on Wednesday, May 28." The CLEBK informed the Council that the hon, secre- tary had written to him stating that among the sub- jects to be discussed at the meeting was the Education Bill,and it was thought desirable to ascertain the opinion of Rural Councils on the following points :—Whether the Council agreed to the additional expense incurred.it being suggested that the rate would be from 4i to 6d ; whether the Council thought the necessary funds or a portion of them should be provided by the Imperial Exchequer ; whether the Bill should be permissive or compulsory ; whether the Councils in districts with a population of 20,000 or even 10,000 should be allowed to become the educational authority ; and whether the Council should have a voice in the opinion of the Ethane- tion Committee. The Clerk was of the opinion that it would be advisable to defer taking action in the matter until after the meeting had been held. It was decided that the Chairman and Clerk should represent the Council at the meeting.

PADGATE SCHOOLS EDUCATION. UNFAVOURABLE REPORT.

The Cmtx intimated that H.M. Inspector had for- warded the following report in reference to the educa- tion of:children at Padgate Schools The upper et indards pas3ed a very fair examinatien. The arithmetic in Standard V is a weak point. Also there is a want of finish and exactitude in much of the work, especially the reading, which no doubt comes tram want of teaching power. Another teacher in the upper school is much needed. The lower standards passed a good examination, but here, too, it would be better if the three monitresses were repla,ed by a teacher. I was pleased with the boys' drill and the girls' needlework, and the industrial work generally is well carried on." OUR LATEST LANCASHIRE CURES. INTENSE SUFFERING A COMPLICATION OF FROM GASTRIC ULCER. TROUBLES. A Liverpool Woman Remarkably Manchester Woman's Recital of a Cured. Notable Cure.

Mrs. Sarah Ann Webster, a woman of forty-one years of rage, who resides at 508, Edge-lane, Liverpool, stated to a reporter that her troubles commenced three years:ago.

_ . "I was taken," she says, "with pains in ray stomach and across my body. I had to go about the kitchen stooping—l couldn't stand up straight. I was that way for months, and it is only through taking Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People that I am strong and well as you see me to-day. I first called in a doctor, who ordered me to bed, and I was in bed three days. It turned out to be ulceration of the coating of the stomach. I could not keep anything on my stomach. It all came back, and I was that weak through lack of nourishment that I couldn't lift my hand up to my mouth. Indeed, it would be difficult to exaggerate the suffering I :experienced, until by good fortune I learned the merits of Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People. The pain I suffered before I knew of them cannot be described. I was under the doctor until I was sick and tired of going, and I have a cupboard full of empty medicine bottles. They eased me for a tinge, you know, aid then I was as bad as ever. The disease, called in medical lan- guage gastric ulcer, arises out of neglected indigestion. Every time I ate anything I suffered in- tense pain until it came back, and then I would be as hungry as ever. But.in time even hunger deserted me for I became very weak and ill through lack Of suffi- cient nourishment, and did not feel like eating. Of course," Mrs Webster went on, " my weakness got I much greater, and, indeed, after a time I couldn't do nay little bit of work,and for three years this went on;it was perfectly miserable for me and everybody." " How did it stop ? Well, I'll tell you. One day, about six months ago, one of my sons read in the paper about a cure by Pr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People. It referred to a person who had the same ail- ment as myself. My husband got a box, and after I had taken four pills I found great relief. I kept on,and I got altogether seven boxes of Dr Williams' Pink Pills, and the last box is only half empty. Dr Williams' Pink Pills acted in a marvellous way on my stomach. They seemed to heal the ulcerated coating, which formerly rejected all food, and as time went on I could take a stronger and stronger diet, without suffering those bad pains. They gave me a good appetite. I can now eat my food and enjoy it, the pain has gone entirely,and now I am as well as ever I was in my life."

At the very end of Upper Brook-street, and facing the park gates is Autumn-street, Manchester. It is the centre of an interesting chain of events which led a reporter to make special inquiries in Autumn-street itself.

No 9 proved to be the residence of Mrs Annie Laura Martin, wife of Mr Alfred Martin, a builder and con- tractor. The story told by Mrs Martin is as follows "About sixteen years ago I began to be very ill. I suffered from indigestion, rheumatism, and a weak heart. I had pains in the chest, and pains between the shoulder blades, and nothing did me good until, after I had been treated by the best doctors in Manchester, I fortunately heard of Dr Williams rink Pills for Pale People.

"My illness began with indigestion, as I have men- tioned. Heart disease developed afterwards. I also suffered greatly from enlargement of the liver." "How long were you under the treatment of the medical men ?"

"Up to about twelve months ago. I have taken more bottles of medicine than I can count, and pills and powders as well ; in fact, I have taken every kind of medicine, including patent medicines also. I ' used to have a splendid set of teeth, but they have gone, and I attribute their loss to the mercurial potions I have swallowed. If I had known of Dr. Williams' Pink Pills earlier I should not have lost my teeth."

"You are looking very well now, Mrs. Martin. What were the circumstances that led up to this happy change ?" "It is a little over a year ago that a pamphlet with reference to Dr Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People was thrust under my door. At the same time my attention was also arrested by reading an article in the "Man- chester Evening News "of the sufferings and cure of a person who had experienced the same dyspepsia and other rheumatic and heart troubles as myself, I showed the article to my husband, and said I had a good mind to try them. He said, Yes, get a box " Now, tall rrie of their effect, and exactly how you went on,"

"1 took six or seven boxes before I could say that I began to bi much better. After fifteen years' chronic suffering the improvement naturally was gradual. But it was also sure. At the time I started taking the pills, I was subject to great langour, lassitude swelling of the feet and hands, pains in the abdomen, and varicose veins. Life was a burden. I used to feel worse when I got up in a morning than when I went to bed at night. House- hold work was out of the question. All that sort of thing used to be done for me. The doctor had told me I had rheumatic gout in my hands. I had also pain in my side, which I used to think was pleurisy." "But tell me the effect of Dr. Williams' Pink Pills. Did you follow the instructions given in each box ?" "I followed the instructions as far as I could. By degrees I began to feel an improvement in my condition. I slept better, my appetite gradually increased, the 'varicose veins went down, and the pains and cramp which I had experienced, slowly ceased to trouble me. 'After the fifth box the feeling of numbness in the body left me, and I could feel myself getting stronger every ' day. Before I began to take Dr. Williams' Pink Pills I was a confirmed invalid Since then I have walked to Ancoats and back, to All Saints' Church, and even to Withington and Didsbury."

Mr Dolan, the contractor for the extension of theu Corporation Baths io. Legh-street, has this week made a start with the work. The extensions include the provision of about sixteen private or slipper baths; two vapour baths, and alterations to the second class bath so as to incrtase its size. Altera- tions will also be made to the old buildings fronting Legh-street. The scheme further includes the erec- tion of a manager's house, comprising two rooms below and two above with a frontage to Bath-street. A new entrance to the baths will also be provided in Bat h•street. The estimated cost of the extensions La £3,804.
