THE EXAMINER.

MAY 23rd 1932.

FEDERATION AND PROTECTION.

Mr. CHAMBERLAIN'S great speech at Birmingbam the other day covered many topics ; and, while 'we are all louking eagerly for some omen of peace it is natural that his remarks "TRANSCENDENT on South Africa should have POSSIBILITIES." excited the most eager atten- tion. He told us little— he is " hopeful but not sanguine" of peace—but he gave evidence of a very new spirit by repudiating unconditional surrender" in set terms, and launching forth into a really generous eulogy of the virtues of the Boers. All this was interest- log, so much so that it has somewhat over - , shadowed the peroration of the speech. But Mr. CHAMBERLAIN'S plea for protection has more than a passing interest. This is a question that has a history. It was just about the time When Mr. CHAMBERLAIN ceased to be a Radical and became a Unionist that the middle classes of Birmingham and the Mid- ands generally publicly abandoned their fai th in 'Free Trade. It was a period of severe indus trial depression, and a Commission was busy investi- gating its causes. The Midlands had their own "I'lB4. The Midlands believe 0-hat "trade follows the flag," and in almost identical to rms the Chambers ef Commerce of Birmingham and Sheffield declared for some Imperial system of protection—a tariff wall round the Empire to keep • foreign products out, and Free Trade within it to assist the' circulation of their own wares in the Colonies. There the question slumbered for a a time, but the evolution of Mr. CHAMBERLAIN continued, until in 1895 he cast the last rags of his Liberalism aside, and joined L 3rd S4A.Lis-

1 . .suss's Cabinet. And then a year later, in m 1896, the man fro Birmit.gham—faithful ixouthpiece of the Midlands—went down to the Congress of the Chambers of Commerce in London and delivered his famous speech in

_ favour of an Imperial Customs Union. "The Empire," he said, " was commerce," and Imperial- ' ism meant for him the acquisition of new markets and the protection of the old. He wanted fedora- . tion on the basis of a preferential tariff. We were to tax foreign wheat, meat, and sugar, while we admitted the Colonial article free,and the Colonies in return, were to open their ports to our mann- faCtUreS and close them to those of our rivals. P e . tnoech fell somewhat flat, 'out Mr. CHAMBERLAIN s tot the man to drop his pat projects. He has used the war to encourage the sentiment of Imperial Unity. Every speech he has made in the last two years has been a veiled plea for Fedora. tion. Then came the Bread Tax, which threw Free Trade to the winds. In a month there is to be a 'conference of Colonial Premiers, which is to dis- cuss the possibility of an Imperial Customs Union, 4t1.4 both Canada and Australia have declared for Preferential trade. The Coronation is the psy- chological moment, and now Mr CHAMBERLAIN has raised his standard once more. He will not be without opposition, even from his own side. Lord SALISBURY has expressed his scepticism very clearly. Speaking to the Primrose League, he said: There aye very important men, men of great intellect And aut b°rltY.who think that the moment has come kr some leßialative action on our part which should federate our Colonieg. . . . The tendenCy • • • Of statesmen . is to think that beeause their own wretch.ed lives are confined to Some sixty or Reventy yeArs it is open fto them to torco an anticipation o the results which the natural play of forces will bring before us. ut opposition is the salt of life to Mr. CHAMBER- -1.141N. He has appealed from Lord SALISBURY to country. "Lab us raise our thoughts," he !aid at Birmingham, "to the transcendent possi- tilities of a federation of the British race." It is

4 highly exciting and piquant situation.

IT is not difficult to understand what is the 1113tive for haste in Mr. CLIAMBEkLAIN'S mind. l'he masterful, vigorous man is no longer young. Lord SALISBURY'S rather morivn FOR bitter reading of his case is HASTE. probably true. He is that most dangerous type of fire- brand, an old man in a hurry. Lord SALISBURY Iltaticis in his path, perhaps, but how long will the W.eary veteran retain the Premiership if a declara- tion of peace should crown his labours at the 3ronatiol. Moreover, the lesson of Bury makes lt clear that the country will not tolerate a return ta Protection merely to bring in a small sum to the areverme,while conferring another dole on the lan- revenue. A slow and insidious undermining of 'reeTrade would-not be easylThe only chance is to

excite the imagination of the country by a grandi- ose dream of a united Empire, to point to Federa- tion as the goal, and induce us to accept an Imperial Preferential Tariff as its necessary con- dition. Then, too, there is always the class which is ready to take alarm at foreign bounties and American trusts. It is to both of these considera- tions that Mr CHAMBERLAIN appealed at Birmingham: It is quite impossible that these new methods of competition can be met by adherence to old and aatiquated methscis. If by adherence to economic pedantry, to old shibbol,ths, we are to lose oppor- tunities of closer union which are offered us by our colonies . . . if we do not take every chance in our power to keep Britiah trade in BriLish hands, we shall deserve the disasters whieh will infallibly come upon us. Free Trade, in short, is a " fly-blown phylactery," Imperial Federation a" transcendent possibility.' FORTUNATELY the Bread Tax has come in time to give the working classes some little foretaste of what',Mr. CHAMBERLAIN'S scheme would mean. Ti; Bread Tax is levied at AN ECONOMIC present on all wheat,grain,aLd RsvourrioN, flour which comes from over-

seas. If we were to exempt Canadian wheat we should have to raise the duty considerably, in order to obtain even the same small revenue. That would send up the price of the quartern loaf, not one halfpenny, but at least a penny, and probably more. We have had a tax on sugar for a year past. If we were to exempt the West Indian article, that tax also would have to be raised, and with it the retail price. Austra- lian meat would claim Protection at the expense of Argentine, and doubtless we should have to Ipay more for ham, butter, and cheese. But that is not all. Mr. CHAMBERLAIN poiuts to the hostility of our rivals. Would an attempt to ex- clude their produce and their manufactures mollify their suspicions of us ? On the contrary, they would at once reply by tariffs which would exclude our manufactures from the United States and the Continent. We might gain the whole trade of the Colonies, bac that would not compen- sate us for the loss of our exports to a single Con- tinental State. To withdraw from competition with the rest of the world, moreover, would be to confess our decadence and to take refuge behind a sort of Chinese wall. It would exaggerate all the defects of insularity and lack of adaptability from which our trade suffers at present. It would spell decay and poverty, economic revolution, and not improbably war. The scheme has only to be understood to be defeated.

Lord Rosebery, who, as Mr. Chamberlain says, "has become a Liberal-Unionist," has written a rather remarkable preface to his recent speeches. In it he repudiates Home Rule not merely for reasons of expediency,but apparently on principle. But he goes mach further. He assures us that he never was a convinced Home Ruler. He quotes a speech which he delivered in 1885, in which he said that the oily solution for Ireland was to treat her like the rest of the country, and to bestow our •system of local government upon her. That, he says was still his real opinion in 1886 when he fol- lowed Mr. Gladstone, and again in 1893. It is his opinion now. The Unionists, he admits, adopted his solution when they established County Coun- cils in Ireland. And yet for fifteen years Lord Rosebery supported the Homo Rule Party and opposed the Unionists with whom he agreed in secret. It is a rather painful confession of levity and insincerity. It is to be hoped that every trade-unionist in the country will note the fate in the House of Commons of an attempt to deal with the situation created by the Taff Vale decision. A motion to the effect that legislation was required "to pre- vent workmen being placed by judge-made law in a position inferior to that intended by Parliament in 1875" was strenuously opposed by the Govern- ment, and defeated by a majority—a remarkably small majority however—of Unionist members. It was clearly the intention of Parliament in 1875 to exempt Trades Unions from the consequences' which would follow if they could sue or be sued at law. A generation later that intention is over- ridden by a judge's ruling. The result is to place the whole future of trade unionisni in jeopardy, and to place workmen's combinations in a position much inferior to masters' organisations. No Union caniisk a strike now without staking all its funds, even its sick and benefit funds,since all it posseses can be made liable for the loss to the employer that is inevitable in a labour war. Masters may combine without such risks. Then again the law of picketing is in a state of hopeless muddle. Sir H Campbell-Bannerman espoused the trade- unionist cause, and it is satisfactory to note that Mr. Asquith and Mr Haldane supported him warmly.

A report such as has been issued by the War- rington Football Club speaks well for the manage- ment during the season just concluded. Not only has a profit been made, but the assets have been considerably increased, notwithstanding the ex- pensive nature of the engagements under the new Northern League which the first team had to fulfil The position of the Club is, indeed, in marked Icontrast to that of similar Northern Union organ- sations. With very few exceptions the, premier. clubs have lost money on the season's working, rand all of them find it a most difficult matter to hold their own in the beat football company. Even the champions, Broughton Rangers—the holders of three cups—are compelled to show a debit balance, while Oldham the opulent have lost £7OO. The members of the Warrington Club have, therefore,good reason to feel satisfied with the way the club has been managed, and equal cause for self congratulation on their membership of such an organization. With the finances in such a healthy condition there is a bright prospect before the Warrington FC. It is money that makes the mare go, and vulgar d is as neces- sary for the maintenance of supremacy in the field of sport as anywhere else.

Those who wore beginning to forget the arbitrary action of the Amalgamated Society of Carpenters and Joiners against a Warrington carpenter will be reminded of the matter by the defensive state- ment read at the Trades Council meeting, and, we imagine, their conviction that justice was done at the assizes will be strengthened thereby. The report issued by the General Secretary of the Society endeavours to acquit the Society of doing the wrong thing by stating the case of the injured workman in the worst light possible. We do not argue for a moment that he acted in the most honourable manner towards the Union or that he did not. Such a question is beside the point. What the action'at the Assizes proved was that the Society had shown a certain amount of vindictive- ness in presenting the plaintiff obtaining employ- ment, and for the pecuniary injury thus done him they have had to pay £lOO damages. There is the same evidence of a desire on the part of the society to punish the individual manifest in the report alluded to, and though there may have been some justification for this feeling there was none for the course • taken. Trades Unions have done incalculable good to the cause of labour, but they must not forget that it is theit dusty to serve the needy rather than to punish those who offend against their rules, by inflicting damage which an assize jury has to be asked to assess.

This, gentle reader, is regarded, in some of our C.llonies at least, as" Vic iziria Day"; our late Queen of blessed memory having been born on the 241 of May,lBl9. All British subjects will be delighted to know that the anniversary of Queen Victoria is being spontaneously observed in every part of the Empire. It would, of course, be easy for the King to proclaim May 24th as a public holiday, end it would then be so regarded; but it is more gratify- ing to find that without any such intimation the day is kept in honour of the good Queen who reigned so long over the British Empire. The Canadian Parliament has already made May 241 a legal holiday, and in Halifax, Nova Scotia, the event is officially celebrated under the title of " Empire Day." The attempt to create an " Em- pire Day " at home has hitherto failed ; not be- cause there is no desire for it but because the date fixed by the Bill introduced for that pur • pose was deemed impracticable, The Natal Government has proclaimed the 24th of May a day of thanksgiving to commemorate the reign of Queen Victoria, and in the capital of Jamaica, of which we now hear so much, both the white and coloured population unite in the celebration. There is in this country a very prevalent feeling that in mem- ory of the late Queen May 24th should be marked by some special ceremony, or by a holiday through- out the whole of the Empire. But there should be complete unanimity as to the day to be chosen, and at present one has seen nothing to suggest that an Imperial holiday would not be welcomed by all the subjects of the King, though the fact that Whitsun- tide falls so near May 24th may be regarded in some quarters as an obstacle to the proposal. As the Americans have an Independence Day, however, Canada has a Dominion Day, and as Australia may soon have a Federation Day, so it is hoped that the British in all parts of the world will have a national holiday; and perhaps, after all, no more felicitous date could te chosen than the date on which our late Sovereign was born.

The good which Royalty may do is illustrated in connection with the Welsh Industries Exhibition at Grosvenor *House. The Exhibition has proved a great attraction, it appears, owing to the visit of the Princess of Wales, who evinces a keen interest in all such movements, and made purchases at every stall. Apparently the present undertakine has been a decided succesq, and the patronage it has received from fashionable and wealthy men and wlmen in the metropolis will serve to populari-e it still further. All parts of the Principality have had a share in the work of the exhibition, and the completeness of the arraneemenis is said to refl-ct the highest credit upon the organising capacity of the ladies and gentlemen who have hal the affair in hand. The promoters of the work are now look- in u forward with much hopefulnesss to the forth- coming exhibition at Liverpool. Of course, the more publicity is given tn the work of the Associa- tion which has this business under its control, the more interestlin it will be manifested by the work- ing classes themselves, for whose benefit the move- ment was originally conceived and inaugurated.

The invitation card for the Coronation received by the Lord Mayors and the Mayors of provincial cities and boroughs is a very artistic production. At the top of the card are the words " Coronation of their Majesties King Edward VI! and (been Alex- andra." The Royal Arms occupy the top co.rirs, with the letters respectively "ER "and "A R." The letterpress of the invitation reads :—"By command of the King, the Earl-Marshal is directed to invite Mr --, the Mayor of --- to be present at the Abbey Church of Westminster on the 20th day of June, 1902. Norfolk, Earl-Marshal." The card has an ornamental border beautifully executed, and is printed in a warm brown tint. The artist's name appears in a corner—Mr G W Eve. In the border the principal features are the Stuart crown, and the rose, shamrock, and thistle. Accompanying the card is a circular, stating that the card for admis- sion ta the Abbey, which is not transferable to anyone, with the carriage ticket and printed instructions, will be sent in due course. For the Mayoress there is a separate invitation.

In some provincial towns the magistrates are so mindful of the future well-being of Youthful Offen- ders that when there is occasion for a remand tbey send them to the Workhouse there to spend the interval in a way that will not familiarise novices in crime with the interior :A the local prison-house. Whether there is a great deal to choose between the Workhouse and the lock-up may be a matter of opinion, but the magistrates mean well, and it is not their fault that some arrangement more nearly approaching the ideal is not provide I by Some other authority. The object of a remand is so;nei,imes to prevent a con viction. In a certain city, which it is needless to mention, the magistrates communicated with the General Purposes Committee of the Corporation in the hope that something might be done in this direction, and while the members of the committee sympathised with the boys and the object in view, their sympathy was not such as to move them to make any recommendation to the Council. Sym- pathy in such cases is often but skin deep, and disappears altogether where its possible effects upon the pocket are concerned. Still there may ' be local authorities with hearts in the right place, and it is to these that those who really care for the future of youthful offenders must look for ion- sideration of a practical kind. The matter is worthy of the attention of private philanthropists, as well as of I,lcal representatives on oublio bodies and is one that has already been too long neglected.

The celebration of the Edwardian Millenary at Kingston-on-Thames on Saturday possesses a pecu- liar appropriateness during the present year. Edward the Elder was crowned at Kingston one thousand years ago, and it is profitable to make even an attempt to span the great gulf of history that intervenes between that more or less primitive ceremonial and that of which Elward VII will be the central figure next month. As the Bishop of Rochester remarked, it is a striking thought that at Kingston, Englishmen were present at the Coronation of an English King a thousand years ago,and that this monarch ascended the throne with the goodwill of the people just as King Edwivd VII. will do a few weeks hence. In 902 as in 1902 the King took up the inheritance bequeathed to him from a noble reign, and then as now monarchy was the emblem and headship of an extending domain and consolidating power. But the heritage of Edward VII. is vastly greater than that which fall to the custolianship of the Anglo- Saxon King. To-day King Edward is also an Emperor, holding sway over one of the mightiest empires the world has ever seen. It is a feature of the memorial unveiled at Kingston on Saturday, which took the form of a rich stained glass window in the Council Chamber. that it shows the arms of King Edward VII. and those of Edward the elder, surrounded by the arms of six other Saxon kings who were crowned in the same town.

Holiday-keepers should feel much obliged to that admirable newspaper, the "Times," for seeking to prove that the weather recently experienced is not at all unusual, but, in fact, quite seasonable. Yet there are those who find it difficult to believe the statement. Cold in May we know is not unusual. " Lingering winter chilled the lap of May" in Shakespeare's time, and baron. that. But week after week of wintry winds and sharp night frosts to within a month of Midsummer must assuredly be a violation of precedeat. Whitsuntide is usually a warm weather festival, but this year it found us endeavouring to take comfort in the prospect of the winds becoming a trifle less bitter and severe than they had already been. Whether the prospect has been realised may well be doubted. Never did pro- vincial cricket open the season with so dismal a record of drawn and abandoned matches, watched during their chequered and uneven course by gloomy and shivering spectators. Possibly the summer may be saviog up its agreeable weather "surprises " and heat for the C)ronation, but, be that as it may, the spring of 1902 has established a reputation which we can only hope may long remain unequalled.

Whitsuntide has by no means passed unshadowed in other ways. The festive season has been signal- ised, indeed, by two serious accidents within the Kingdom, and very untoward events abroad, The scene of one accident was in the neighbourhood of the incomparable Lakes of Killarney, where, by a boating mishap thirteen lives were lost ; and the other was near Kilmarnock in Scotland, where a well-laden railway train was derailed and much injury inflicted on passengers, but,happily with no of life. Then the minds of stay-st-bome folks have been upset by reading of a terrible di.zaster in the Gulf of Mexico, and of hay.,c played in the Karachi country by a fierce hurricane which swept away no less than forty miles of the Scinde Rail way.

VACCINATION.

The Rugby Union requested the Runcorn Union to present to the Local Government Beard a memorial stating that the present system of re-vaccination entailed unnecessary expense, and that there was no reason why persona desiring re-vaccination at the public expense should not attend at the surgeries of he public vaccinati-rs, or at some other appointed place, or them- selves defray the cost of the public vaccinator's journeys to their own hoe. Many persons sought re-vaccina- tion by the public vaccinator, not to avoid personal expense, but in order to secure the performance of the operation with lymph supplied by. the Board. The memorialists therefore prayed that the Bard would issue an ceder providing that the fees payable out of the rates for re-vaccination at the homes of the persons vaccinated shall be the same as those for re-vaccination elsewhere than at such homes, and that the public vaccinators shall be entitled to fees for their visits from the persons re-vaccinated at their own homes ; and further that the lila ril would undertake the gratuitous i supply of lymph for primary and secondary vaccinations by private medical practitioners. It was unanimously resolved, on the motion of Mr. Woodward seconded by Mr Ant wise and supported by Dr Gastaldi, to forward a similar memorial to the Local . Government Board.

POLLUTION OF STREAMS-A THREAT.

Mr Reginald Potts, clerk to the Caeshire County Council,writing on the 15:h May, intimated that he had been directed by the Rivers Pollution Committee to in- form the /I.iral Council that act;on must be taken by them to stnp the pollution complained of by the County Medical Officer otherwise the Committee would recom- mend the County Council to sanction proceedings being taken against them. THE EDIJC&TION BILL.

A. circular was received from the Rural District Councils' Association asking the following questions in order to elicit the opinion of the Runcorn Council in common with other COUTICif3 :—" Whether your Council approve of the additional ()spew that will be incurred in maintaining the voluntary schools being thrown upon the rates. It is uncertain what the expense will be but it has been suggested that the rate will probably be from 41 to 61 ; or whether your Council think that the necessary funds should be provided by the Imperial exchequer; (2) Whether you think that the Bill should remain permissive or whether it should be made com- pulsory ; (3) Whether you think that Rural District Councils with a population of, say, over twenty or even ten thousand should be allowed to become the education authority themselves ; (4) Whether you think that Rural Councils should hove a voice in the appointment of the Education Committee. On the motion of Mr Wrignt,seconded by Mr Williams the following members were appointed a special com- mittee to consider and answer the questions prepounded: —Messrs Chadwick,Clark,Woodwsrd,Linsker, Williams, Wright, Pilling, Darlington, Balshaw, Roylance, Stokes and Mclntyre.

COMPLAINTS FROM NO RTRW [CIL

Mr A Fletcher, clerk to the Northwich Roral District Council. wrote under date May 13m, stating that at a meeting of that body held on Friday attention was drawn by the Medical Officer of Health to the fact that sewage from the Runcorn Rural Council's district was allowed to enter the brook at Crowton known as Stoney brook. This brook was used by the cottagers in Crowton for slopping and general purposes about the house, and it was exaemely important that it should be kept free from sewage pcllutlon. The Northwich authority was quite certain that the Run- corn Rural Council would rmder assistance by taking care that all cause of complaint was removed. The attention of the Council tad been called to it in conse- quence of a case of typhoid fever at a cottage where the water was used.

Inspector Farrington was instructed to inspect the stream and report.

EFIE' FT FROM A WIDNES 60TEL.

Before Messrs T Cooke, T 11 Thomas, and T S Timmis, at Widnes, on Thursday, George Wills, a stranger, was charged with stealing 20s from the bar ot the Arch Hotel, on the 14th inst., the money of W Machin. Superintendent Keighley stated that prisoner came into the bar, and asked for a pint of beer, offering a penny, and was refused. Shortly afterwards the landlord turned to go into the tap- room, when Wills snatched up a packet containing 20s. Chase was given, and prisoner caught three hundred yards away, and given into custody of Police-constable Dickson. Upon being charged, he said . "I had to do it." A list of convictions in other towns was recorded, and the bench seat him to prison for three months. RUNCORN WOMAN'S ATTEMPTED SUICIDE.

Northwich. on Moaday, Annie Richards. aged fifty-two, a native of Runcorn, married woman, living apart from her husband, was charged with attempting to commit suicide. The evidence showed that on Saturday afternoon two men named Nutter and Lloyd found her lying unconscious face downwards in the brook behind Nortn wich Vicarage They rescued her, but she broke away, and at- tempted to jump into the river Dane, crying, " Let me go into the water." Canstable Roberts said she was in drink. She pleaded guilty, and was re- manded until Tuesday. When the prismer was brought before the Court on Tuesday it transpired that she wee a housekeeper for twelve years to Mrs Reynolds, Runcorn. For two years she lived in Northwich. The prisoner was repentant, and was handed over to Mrs Reynolds. -

DEATH OF A LATE RUNCORN LADY.

It is with —Lei; regret t7h-at'W-ji-i;av—e—^tTrecord this week the death of Nlri Shipley, nee Alice Cooke, of Jeppestown, South Africa, late a resident of this town, at the early age of twenty.fout. It will be remembered by our readers that it is haraly twelve months since her wedding, wnich was one of the most popular ever solemnized in local Wesleyan Methodist circles. The deceased young lady prior to her marriage and removal to South Africa was a foremost worker in connection with St Paul's Wesleyan Church and Brunswick Sunday School. She was also a member of the choir and was connected with many other similar org inizations. Two months after her marriage with Mr Shipley, a South African gentleman, she set sail for Johannesburg. Nothing of an untoward character was heard about Mrs Shipley's health and it was confidently antici- pated that the climate would have no deleterious effect upon her conr,itution. On Sunday however, the Rev J B Foster received a letter from the Wesleyan minister in Johannesburg acquainting him with the circumstance of Ars Shipley's some- what sudden demise on the 23rd April. The news was conveyed with all possible speed to her father. Mr Cooke, registrar, by the Rev J B Forster. The news soon was noised abroad and in the evening the rev gentleman after his sermon at ISt Paul's referred to the sad event. The announce- ment came with terrible suddenness to many in the ' congregation and there were visi'olo signs of emotion as the organist, Mr if W Handley, played the Dead March in -qaul. Everyone who knew the late Mrs Shipley will regret that such a useful aid beautiful life has been so prematurely ended. The greatest sympathy is felt for her sorrowing relatives

RUNCORN NOTES.

[BY A CORRESPONDENT.] Whitsuntide has hardly been marked in Runcorn with such an amount of festivity as is usually the case. Though the friendly societies did not make their usual demonstration the streets all day were crowded with people. On Whit Tuesday the , weather opened out finely and great numbers left the town for various resorts. Toe bookings at the railway station were in excess of last year and ttie town wore quite a deserted appearance by reason of the exodus.

One Runcorn party had a very narrow escape in their wanderings from home. The annual trip of the St Paul's choir this year was fixed for Rhyl, and wbiie in this pretty seaside resort the members had a most exciting adventure which, happily, just missed disastrous results. The day's pro- gramme included a drive out of the town to St Asaph. The party were conveyed in two large vehicles, the foremost of which held about twenty persons.. All went well until the party was nearing home. When in sight of the Salisbury Hotel, one of the three horses slipped down. The coachman rising in his seat to rein the horse up from its haunches, unluckily forgot that tie had his foot on the brake spring, which being slightly relieved of his pressure, when he stood up hurled him over the seat under the horses feet. The reins slipped from his grasp and the horses startled at the coachman's fall, at once started off at a mad gallop. Luckily, however Mr Harry Dodd, Church-street, who happened to be on the box with the driver, managed at great per- sonal risk, to lean over the side of the vehicle and pick the reins out of the horses feet while the horses were at full speed. Rezaining his position on the box Mr Dodd and Captain Anderson managed to turn the horses round just as they were making for a pablichoue which stood on the corner of the road. Before, however, they were brought to a standstill the vehicle collided with great force with ens of the trees surrounded by iron railings which line the thoroughfare an I which were completely uprooted. Beyondiexperiencing a fright none of the members of the party were hurt save the coachmen whose thighs were run over aftor he had fallen over the box. No bones, strange to say were broken, and it is fortuna:e that the in- cident closed as it did for the streets were crowded with people at the time of the accident.

Anent the recent disaster at Killarney it is not generally known that Mr Watson of Norman-road, Runcorn, had a very providential escape. Mr Watson was attached to the party in Ireland and was asked by some of the members of the party te join in the proposed pleasure trip on the lake. It is stated that Mr Wacson replied that though he was sorry to spoil the harmony of the party he belonged to a sect—the Wesleyan Methodist—which did not countenance Sunday pleasure. The reaoon was accepted and the party set sail without. him. A ohort time after every one of Mr Watson's party was drowned

[Br TRIBUNE'S:I. lam told that my few notes lest week on the spirit of clericalism have caused quite a flutter in the clerical dove cote. I am also informed that, with their usual cunning, the article is fathered on a prominent Runcorn Nonconformist., who boasts that he can trace his connection with Methodism to the time of John VI esley. This idea is quite erroneous, however. There is every reason to believe that the Clerical party would be only too pleased if they could delude the townsfolk with the idea that this hatred ,of clericalism springs from one man only. Those who believe such groundless assenions will have a rude awak- ening some day. The vital against clerical- ism is not the work of one or two, but is rather a combined movement of the people's will against clerical predominance. Sorry indeed am I to strip the plumes from the gentleman referred to, but out of fairness to him it ought to be stated that " Tribunus "is one who, being baptised into the truly orthodox church, claims to receive all the blessings derivable from the doctrine of Baptismal regeneration.

On Whit Mondays Runcorn presented a spectacle over which anaels must have wept. In the after- noon there were actually three Sunday school parades—the Dissenters, the Anglicans and the Romanists. How pitiable it all is that a small town ' like Runcorn should be torn by religious differences. Instead of one united Sunday School demonqtration men's minds are unsettled by the apparent want of charitableness between the churches. Truly the meek and lowly spirit of Christ is all but an ' unknown quality in this little dogmatic town of ours.

Doubtless many an outsider, who hears of the religious fracas %mong the churches asks what is the cause? The question is a pertinent one and requires answering. Up to some few years ago the Anglican and the Dissenter lay down in on pasture in, figuratively speaking, each other's bosom. One church could not do too much for its neighbour, and each professed respect for the other's prin- ciples if not downright love for its service. How the chanir,e has been wrought must be found out by everyone for himself, but, to use a Weller- ism, it would be a very easy matter for anyone to guess at the root cause of all the evil three times and miss twice on purpose.

All ratepayers must sincerely wish that the Coro- nation proceedings will mark a fresh departure in Runcorn annals. If all the Sunday schools join together once in a town's proce.sion why can it not be done every year at Whitsuntide? If Mr Taylor does nothing else under his chairmanship he will earn the eternal gratitude of all ratepayers for bringing about such a happy consummaticn.

Since writing last week the ballot for the various schools has 13eu declared. Really it seems as though there was a divinity which shaped the end as well as the beginning of the list. ' Old Bruns- wick" leads the van while the:Parish:Church brings up the rear. The following is the order :- 1 Brunswick 11 Halton Road 2 Trinity 12 Primitives 3 Camden 13 St. Edwards 4 St Michael's 14 St.:John's 5 Hope Hall 15 Bethesda 6 Ashridge-street 16 Nelson-Areet 7 Mill Brow (Ragged) 17 Me-sey Mission 8 Welsh 18 St. Luke's 9 Mainers 19 Parish 10 Salvation Army 20 Ellesmere-street

Brunner, Mond, and Co.

On Saturday Messrs Brunner, Mond, and Co. iFsued their annual report and balance-sheet. It set fort that the prafit and loss account showed a bale ce on the working of the year ended March 31 last of £480,431, which,with the amount, of £36,930 brought forward, made a total of £517,361. The directors proposed to deal with this balance as follows : Interim half-yearly dividend on the pre- ference shares at 7 per cent paid on December 6, 1901. 227,249; dividend now to be paid en the preferetce capital, £27,249 ; interim half-yearly dividend on the ordinary capital at 30 per cent,paid on December 9 last, £163,6b4; dividend now to be paid on the ordinary capital, making 324 per cent for the year, £199,007. The amount to be placed to the suspense account is £60,000. the amount to be written off patents account £2,500, and the balance to be carried forward £37,000. The directors record with regret the death of Mr John Crosfield. a direc- tor of the Company since its inception, and chairman of the Board during the first ten years. The directors who retire by rotation and are eligible for re-election are Mr Milner, Mr Galloway, Dr Hewitt, Mr 11 Brunner, and Mr Bell.

Funeral of a Balaclava Hero. At Widnes on Tuesday there were interred the remains of Thomas Wright, of Major Cross-street, Widnes, and who formerly lived in Warringtongene of the famous Six Hundred. A crowd of about 200 assembled at the Fernworth Churchyard, where the funeral teok place. Heading the cortege were hr George Rogerson and Colour-Sergeant O'Brien, the former wearing on hie breast Crimean end Turkish medals. Then followed a posse of police, under Sergeants Radcliffe and Robinson. Included in the procession were also the ex-Mayor (Alderman G I Neil), Mr J Carroll, Mr ~,howm ii, Mr A Poulson, &c. On the coffin was the inscription :—" Thomas Wright, died llith May, 1902, aged 71 years." The veteran was a great favourite in the town, and was known by the name of " Old Tom," and many were the tears shed as his remains were consigned to their last resting-place. among the wreaths was an exceedingly pretty one from the Widnes police. The funeral service was (by the deceased's ex- pressed wish) conducted by the Rev J Adams, M.A., curate of St Ambroie), who in an excellent address, made affecting allusion to the old soldier's admirable character, and expressed the hope that a stone would he erected in his honour.

FDRMAI lON OF _A GARDEN

SOCIETY At-Sl-0-6KtcTii-fiE-ATH.

A public meeting was held in the Church Room, Stockcon Heath, on Thursday last week to consider the advisability of forming a Stockton Heath Garden Society The Vicar (Rev Ei V Pigot) presided over a good atten- dance.

The Chairman said their idea was to encourage the inhabitants of the parish to a greater interest in their gardens, and in other people's gardens, so that by minuet encouragement the appearance of the village might be promoted and improved, and also that the character of the people who took part in the work might be promoted and improved, because he thought garden- ing was wholesome from every point of view. (Hear, hear.) It was healthy physically and morally, and if they could get the inhabitants of the village back to the land, as the saying was new-a-days, and to take an interest in the earth and its produce, they would have done something which would bear grad fruit. (Hear, hear.) The first mention of the proposed garden society came from Mr Jenkins at a meeting held recently in connection with their corona- tion celebration. That had now taken definite form, an Mr Jenkins had started independent of the corona- tion though he (the speaker) thought ttat if it grew to a great success in the fame, they would always connect it in their mind with that very memorable year. The , present meeting was the result of deliberations held at the residence of Mr Jenkins, 83 that Mr Jenkins wao at the bottom of it all. Mr Woodcock, also, had the , matter array near his heart, and had a claim upon theie gratitude tor the pains he had taken to get things intr ehape for that evening's meeting. All present were, he ventured to think, enthusiastic lovers of the garden,end , the scheme was to increase that love and enabee them to he'p each other. (Applause.) Mr, Jenkins said that the idea was co-operation and , mutual beaefit, and a general desire to preserve the rural character of the neighbourhood and its surround- ings, which must be bound to have a beneficial effect, from many points of view, on the inhabitants. (Hear, , hear). Mr. Jenkins mentioned that they had applied ! for, and recetved, valuable information as to working from various other garden societies, so that so far as ! machinery was concerned, they were ready to put the ' proposal into shape, if they could get a sufficient warranty for doing 60. If the idea should take root he ' should be glad to refleet that he bad done something to encourage a development which tended to beautify life. (Applause).

• 1 liefr W fi Woodcock said the objects of the proposed Societywere to encourage a love of gardening ; to pre- serve and improve the rural character of the village ; mutual assistance to develop and improve gardens ; to obtain good seeds and distribute to those who required them ; the mutual exchange of plants, seeds, ko. They had purposely avoided calling it a Cottage Garden Society, for they thought that would savour too much of patronage—the patronage of the villa resident for the cottager. Not that such a distinction was necessary,for seine of the most lovely gardens in England were cottage gardene. Over the porches, they could see clematis,and rose, and canary creeper, and honeysuckle, and in the beds sweet williams, peonies, hollyhocks, sun- flowers, and pansies ; and the little wayside plots which cheered the heart of the labourer as he Came home from his daily toil were as full of b iauty as they well could be, and cheered the heart of the passer by as well as thattof the owner. It would certainly he necessary for the scciety some day to define the two classes, vet they intended to make the line of demarcation as fine as possible,for their purpose was—and he hoped they would continually keep it before them—to instil into the hearts of all residents of Stockton Beath a love of horticulture —a love of plant and flower growing—and in so doing to cultivate and foster a mutual respect for all within the bounds of the society, and a spirit of mutual help- fulness, which he thought could only be of benefit to all concerned and would do so much to elevate and lift up the status of the inhabitants of the (Hear, hear). He should like to see all the householders of Stockton Heath joining, and though a first sight they might not think it, yet it was possible for all such to participate in the society's work, for it was actually not neeeesary for them to occupy a single rood of ground. If they had neither front garden nor back, neither fruit garden, nor flower garden, nor cab- bage garden, but only a well-equipped window, and a single flower pot, a place could De found fee them in the society, and they would be eligible for one at least of the prizes which they hoped to offer for compEtition. (Hear, hear). What they wanted the inhabitants to de. was to cultivate a love fora garden, whether it be a cottage or a villa garden, a front garden or a back gar- den, or even a single plant in a solitary flower pot, but whatever it was let them learn to love it for what it was, and take a pride in growing well whatever they chose to grow, and tia realise that a well-grown cabbage, or potato. or onion, was in itself a thing to be aimed at and attained only by eareful thoughtful work—plotting and planning, knowing how to do a certain thing and when to do ie. La much true pleasure and profit might be got froen tne thereugh culture of thistles and brambles (there were Epeciel of both well worth growing in any garden) as from the rarest !orchid owned by the millionaire. (Applause.) There lwere also many examples amo og elents of those that were not only sen- sitive to the toech, but which also possessed the power of catching living insects and putting them te death for the purpose of food. There were °there so low in the scale of life that they mid hardly be said with cer- taiety to be either plant sir anim 11. Then there was the story of what particular part plants played in the economy of nature. why plants develop d colour and how they did so, how they dietilled their sweet odours. and why they did so, how their forma and organs were modified, and for what purpose. The study of these things were all Within the scope of that society, and the Executive Committee would no doubt see that these features were not overlooked. Mr Woodcock gave inter- esting illustrations of the work the Society could do, ' and in conclusion expressed the hope that all who joined the Society would learn the lessons and the parablee of nature, for there were, as Shakespeare said, Tongues in trees, Books in the running brooks, Sermons in stones And good in everything.. Mr England moved that a society be formed, and that it be Called the Stockton Heath Garden Sxnetv. Ile remarked that he would be glad to offer a prize for the cottage section. (Hear, hear.) Air Timperiey seconded and the motion was unani- mously agreed to. _ _

Tho-following committee was elected, with power to add :—The Rev H V Pigot, Messrs Woodcock, A J Willett, S F Welsby, Vernon, R T Westbrook, J Ryan, J Moore, and A Broady. The Rev H V Pigot was elected president, Mr W H J Jenkins hon eecretary, and Mr T R Westbrook bon treasurer, On the motion of Mr fl Isherwood, seconded by Mr Frank Warburton, it was resolved that the sutscriptiou for membership shouli be one shilling annually. The meeting clo-sed with a cordial vote of thanki to the Rev. II V Pigot for presiding.

INSTITUTION AND INDUCTION OF THE REV. W. BRACECAMP.

A special service was held in St, Paul's Church on Friday evening for the institution and induction of the new vicar. Rev. W. Bracecamp, to the benefice. There was a large congre- gation, and many of the local clergy were present. The sermon was preached by the Lord Bishop of Liverpool. His Lordship based his discourse on Ephesians iv, verses 8, 11 and 12. The Rev. W. Bracecamp, having taken the usual oaths, was then institued by the Bishop. After prayer had been engaged in, the new vicar was inducted to the living by the Yen. Archdeacon Madden, and tolled the church bell to signify his taking possession. The offertory was on behalf of the Endowment Fund.

Honorary Ranks in the Army for Local Volunteer Officers.

Friday night's " London Gazette," contained the names of a number of officers of the Volunteer companies for service in South Africa who on hav- ing relinquished their temporary commissions,have been granted honorary ranks in the Army, with permission to wear the uniform of their companies. CLAIM FOR COMMISSION.

A Warrington Firm Sued in the County Court.

===== 0002601_19020523_art0112.txt | included=true | included =====
A Disputed Telephone Communication.

At the Warrington County Court on Thursday, last week before his Honour Judge Bowen Rowlands, 8.C., George,Fuller,4o,Brazenome-street,lldanohester, brought an action against Messrs Skelton's Ltd, Warriegtoss to recover £33 10e, commission alleged to be due to him for negotiatiag a loan for the defendants amounting to £4,soo—Mr Ambrose Jones,harrister, (instructed by Mr Watson, Manchester) appeared for the plaintiff, and Mr Rutledge, barrister, Liverpool, (instructed by Messrs Davies and Forshaw) represented the defendants. Mr Jones, in opening the case, said his client was a 4 surveyor and agent, and part of his business was to negotiate loans for persons who desired to raise money on mortgage. About the beginning of this year the defendants were desirous of raising a loan for £4.50e, to be secured on a mortgage on their property in Warring- ton. They placed the matter in the hands of their solicitors, Messrs Davies and Forshaw, who in the course of their inquiries,made for the purpose of bringing about the loan, became acquainted with a Mr Wilson, who carried on business in Manchester. This gentle- man suggested to the plaintiff that he would perhaps be able to negotiate the loan for the defendants: On the 4th February Mr Fuller wrote to Messrs Davies and ' Forshaw and stated that his terms for the negotiating of the loan would be £l2l 5s and disbursements, made up at (1) mortgagee's solicitor's costs for procuring the mortege, £33 15s ; (2) perusing title, £42 10s, and (3) plaintiff's conarniseion at the ordinary rate of one per cent, £45, Messrs Davies and Forshaw replied on the sth February to the effect that their clients considered the costs excessive and would not be prepared to pay anything like that amount. Further correspondence took place and as a result plaintiff came ever to War- rington on the 12th February to attend a meeting of the directors of Skelton's Limited. At that time the question of costs seemed to be atilt open but Mr For- sh tw authorieed the plaintiff to proceed withthe negotia- ting of the loam As a result of this,plaintiff corresponded with a ;firm of solicitors in Manchester, Messrs Mann and Rooke, who tell him that they had a client who had the money, and would be prepared to advance the same if the security was satisfactory. In consequence of this, Mr Fuller came over and inspected the property, and recommended it as a satisfactory investment. When the question of costs was discussed, Mr Forshaw said he was prepered to take his chance at to what he should get from his clients in the matter. Subse- quently Mr Fuller saw Messrs Mann and Rooke and came to an arrangement that they should waive their procuration fee (£33 153) and he (plaintiff) would accept that amount from the defendants as his corn. mission. The whole cost of the transaction, including £47 for perusing the title, and stamps, would then be £BO 153. On February 20th, Messrs Davies and Forshaw wrote to the plaintiff stating that the , question of costs was satisfactmily arranged Plain- tiff then verete to Messrs Mann and Rooks stating that the whole thing was now atranzed, and everything was ready to he put through. Mr Fuller subsequently saw Mr Wilson who told him that he had heard that the whole matter would be put before a meeting of directors so that they could confirm the action of their solicitors. On:Februaryl2stle a letter wasreceived from Messrs Mann and Rooke Raping that the money was in band, and on February 26th Messrs Davies and Forshaw informed the plaintiff that the directors had refused to take up the loan. That was the first he heard of any repudiation of the arrangements which had been made by defendants' solicitors. Plaintiff wrote to Messrs Davies and For- shaw to the effect that as he had proeured the loan he would look to them for his commission. They replied that no terms had been agreed upon and that plaintiff was not instructed to obtain the money. Although plaintiff went over to Warrington to inspect the pre- perty and although Mann and Rooka had the money ready to advance, defendants turned round at the last moment and said plaintiff had no authority to get the loan. He (Mr Jones) submitted that plaintiff had ful- filled his part of the bargain and was entitled to com- mission. _

Plaintiff was then called, and stated that on the 15th February Mr Forshaw wrote informing him that the direetors had decided that the costs (£l2l ss) were too great to allow the matter to go through, but if the plaintiff could reduce the solicitor's costs and the pro- curation fee. he thought the matter would go through. He and Mr Forshaw continued to correspond, and on the 17th February he wrote to Mr Forshaw complaining that he (Mr Forshaw) did net make any definite offer regarding the coats. He (plaintiff) arranged with Messrs Mann and Rooke that they should waive their procuration fee which should be handed over to the plaintiff as his commission. This brought the costs including the procuration fee and the Perusing fee down to £BO 153. A. day or two before the 20th February Mr Forshaw rang him up and said the directors would accept the reduced costs and that he (Mr Forshaw) would try and get the procuration fee increased to one per cent. He (witness) considered this a binding instruction and at once wrote Messrs Mann and Rooks, saying that the arrangements were corn- plated. In oross-examination plaintiff denied that at the cernmencement of the negotiations Mr Forshaw told him that the director.; would not entertain the matter until they knew what the whole of the coste would be. Mr Forshaw did not say that the directors would never I think of paying the full scale of costs, on the ' contrary he said they would pay it. On February 18th or 19th Mr Forshaw telephoned to him to sev that his clients were prepared to pay the solicitors' iteveetigating fee, and the negotiating fee, which he would try to get increased to one per cent, and he asked plaintiff if the clatter could be carried through. Plaintiff replied that he thought it could. For the defence Mr Rutledge said the plaintiff's state- ment as to the telephonic communication on February 1813 or 19th was the statement oa which his case would either stand or fall. His (Mr Rutledge'a) case was an absolute denial that Such a communication took place. He pointed out that in a letter on February 21st plaintiff made no reference whatever to the alleged telephonic communication. Mr W J Forshaw, solicitor, gave evidence and denied telling the plaintiff that the directors would pay the full scale of charges. He told him that the directors must I know what the full costs would be before they would entertain the matter. Plaintiff attended a directors' meeting and when he said that the cost would he £l2l the directors told him that they would net pay any such sum. Plaintiff said he would like the matter to go thrnueh if it were possible and the directors said they would let him know their decision in a few dare. Witness communicated with plaintiff on February 15th by telephone, and told him that the charges submitted were to high and that he was instructed to tell him that the matter was " off." He also sent plaintiff a letter to the same effect. He never told the plaintiff that defendants were prepared to nay the solicitors' inves- tigating fee, and the negotiating fee, and that he would try to get the latter increased to one per cent. He had no entry in his books of the telephonic communica- tion.

Mr W Skn!ton. a directnr of the defendant company, said plaintiff's offer was rejected'ewing to the amount of the exoensep. _ . _

Mr Rutledge submitted that plaintiff had not one tittle of corroboration of his version of the case, and that he had been too premature in arranging for the luau. Mr N Jones again addressed the Court. His Honour said the difficulty was that both parties were gentlemen of the highest reapectabilitv, and were beyond any suspicion of having stated what they knew was not the case. He did not think the plaintiff's evi- dence, given in perfectly good faith, was tufficient to be relied upon to show that a binding and definite agreement was arrived at. In face of the subsequent letters written by the plaintiff he thought Mr Fuller must have misapprehended the intention and effect of the telephonic communicat;oo. Re gave judg- ment for the defendants. but with regard to the costs, plaintiff must pay nothing except the bare taxed costs that be was bound to pay.

Local Delegates at Conferences. The annual congress of the Co-operative Union is being held this week at Exeter, where more than 1,000 delegates aseembled. At a meeting on Mon- day the president gave an address and spoke on the progress of the movement. The representatives of the Warrington branch are Messrs Rathbone, Jeffrey, Critchlay and Dunbobbin.—Mr E Pester is the Warrington representative at the annual meet- '' of the National Independent Order of Odd- fellows which commenced on Monday in the Friendly and Trade Societies Club, Huddersfield.

fatterg to the Ebttor.

A Point for the Police.

Sir.—May I be allowed through the medium of your columns to protest agsinst the scorching of certain cyclists along the Liverpool-road frem Sankey to Bold Bridge. On Wedeesday night a party of these law- breakers deliberately charged the rear of the Lovely-lane Cycling Club, dashing into them at the rate of twenty miles an hour without either ringing their bells or giving warning of any kind. An accident was just prevented by the skilful steering of one of toe members.—Trustiog this may noet the attention of some oue in authority, I am, yours &c., A AESIBER OF THE L.L.C.C.

NOTES ON UAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Cricket.

Cricket matches in this district were brought to a premature conclusion on Saturday by the heavy rain. Warrington visited Liverpool to encounter the Northern eleven and after half an hour's play rain stopped the game for half an hour, whilst at half-past five when Northern had declared their innings closed, rain came down in torrents and the match was eventually abandoned.

A capital start was made by the Rev J Burrough and B. K Fernie against the bowling elf Lynes and W H Podmore. The wicket was very fast and with the boundaries being small, runs earnelat a fast rate. J W Bibby relieved Podmore at 35, whilst Connor took the ball from Lynes at 50. The last-named soon met with success getting Fernie caught at cover slip, whilst at 64 Burrough was taken by Callon in the slips.

Another excellent stand was made by E L Pick and W Fraser, the pair adding 43 before Lynes broke through Pick's defence. Podmore resumed at 120 and met with remarkable success, taking two wickets in his second over and getting E S Pilling, caught at deep square leg. With the score at 133 for six wickets the innings was declared closed, but the players had only reached the pavilion when rain fell very heavily and further play was impossible.

Cycling. About a dozen members of the Warrington Cycling Club spent the Whitsuntide holidays at Glyndyfrdwy, which is situated about midway up the beautiful vale of Llangollen. The weather was anything but springlike, frequent falls of hail and snow taking the place of what would have been preferred, namely, brilliant sunshine. In spite of these drawbacks a very enjoyable week-end was spent in visiting all the attractive spots, viz., Llan- gollen, Carrog, &c. The party arrived in Warrington in good time on Monday night satisfied with the all-too- brief holiday. Another small party of the club spent the holidays in the neighbourhood of Farndon and Overton-on-Dee,and from reports to hand seem to have enjoyed themselves fairly-well.

On Monday afternoon the management of the New Brighton Tower Company arranged an attractive pro- g,ra lime of cycle races, including scratch, handicap, and novice events, for all of which there was a capital entrance. We notice that in the mile handicap, J A Watson, of Warrington, was the fastest heat winner, his time being 2mins, 9-45 secs.

In a cycle race for a 100-guinea cup at Plymouth, on M nday, a sensational accident occurred, five men being seriously injured.

A donkey, yoked to a coster's barrow, was ambling along unconcernedly near Aldgate Station, London, on Saturday, when a venturesome youth on a bicycle endeavoured to cross in front of it. The enterprise was disastrous. The donkey's fore foot got into the front wheel, his hind feet smashed the back wheel, and,the youth lay sprawling in the road. He was picked up by sympathetic spectators. To disentangle the animal was more difficult, but the task was accomplished finally by turning it on its back and, picking _the remains of the bicycle from its feet.

A shocking cycling fatality occurred on Monday, resulting in the death of a solicitor's clerk, named Cyril Robert Emitt, son of a coal merchant's agent living at Holbeach, Lincolnshire. He was cycling into the station yard, and at the same time a cart heavily laden with coal was coming out. The cyclist, to avoid a collision, turned sharply, with the result that he was pitched off, and fell under the cart, The wheels passed over his head and body, death being instantaneous.

In connection with the Coronation festivities at Bristol, the cyclists will head the procession. The Lord Mayor has intimated that the cyclists must not exceed 300, because of the huge size of the procession. £25 will be distributed equally between lady and gentleraan riders for the best decorated machines. Riders are to be in cycling costume, and not fancy dress.

A new handle bar for racing men has been recently patented. In place of the usual cork handle, the entiro bar is covered with a layer of cork, thus affording many comfortable chartgoa nf grip.

According to the " Financial News" the output of cycles at Coventry and Birmingham is larger than at any time since the boom in 1896.

Foot Racing.

At the Sefton Amateur Athletic and Cycling Sports held on Monday, L Green, of Sankey Harriers (15) came in third in the 220 yards fiat race (open). In the one mile flat race (open) J Ditchfield, Widnes (130),obtained second position, and J Welsh, St Helens (120), was third.

J Moran, of Warrington (12k) won the third prize in the 220 yards flat race at Wigan Infirmary sports on Monday. D Gibson, of Latchford, finished first in the obstacle race, but was disqualified.

Cheshire Volunteers in , Camp. During the week the Cheshire Volunteer Infantry Brigade has been encamped on Perham Down, Salisbury Plain, and will remain there until Sun- day. The various detaehments assembled at their head quarters on Sunday morning, and proceeded by special trains to Salisbury Plain. The Lymm Company includes several Warrington men; and is under the command of Captain Groves. The total strength of the Brigade is as follows :—Brigade Staff, Officers 4, N.C.O.'s and men 5, Total 9 • let V.8.C.R., Officers 26, N.C.O.'s and men 475, total ' i0l; 3rd V.S.C.R.,Officers 18, N.C.O.'s and men, 689; total 707; 4th V.8,C.R., Officers 31, N.O o.'s and men, 781, total.Bl2 ;sth V. B.C.R.,officers 26,N.0 o.'s and men, 792, total 818; Brigade Bearer Co. Officers 1 ; N.C.O.'s and men, 40, total 41 ;grand total 2,888. Many new features have been included in the week's work at camp. Sale of Woolaton Property. At the Blue Bell Inn, Warrington, on Wednesday evening, Messrs John Mainwaring and Company offered ifor sale by auction a freehold fully licensed hotel, freehold dwelling houses, blacksmith and wheelwright's shops,freehold building sites and farm lands at Woolston, near Warrington. Lots 1 and 3 were offered together. They consisted of,the freehold and fully-licensed hotel, known as the Rope and Anchor, at Woolston, together with the yard, stabling, bowling green, garden, and other conven- iences thereto, in the occupation of Mr Henry Daintith. Also the wheelwright's shop adjoining on the north,leased for ten years from let January, 1895, at £5 a year. The hotel and premises are leased to Messrs Greenall,Whitley and Co., Limited, for seven years from the let September, 1898, at £6O a year, superficial area, 2,904 4-9 square yards. In addition there was also offered the plot of free- hold building land, in the occupation of Mr James llepherd, situate on the northerly side of and ad- ' joining the Rope and Anchor Hotel, with an exten- sive frontage to Dam-lane, superficial area 873 2-3 [ square yards. Bidding commenced at £2,000 and after a brisk competition reached .£3,400 at which figure it was sold to Messrs Greenall, Whitley and Company. The next lot offered comprised two freehold cottages, with the blacksmith's shop, yard, and premises fronting the high road, gross rental, .£25 12s a year ; super- ficial area, 643 49 square yards. .£l5O was the sum at which bidding commenced and £3OO was reached before Mr John Simonett of Warrington became the purchaser. Lot 4 consisted of two freehold semi- detached dwelling-houses, with the outbuildings, gardens, and other conveniences fronting tp main high road. Each house contains sitting-reom, parlour, kitchen,and out offices on the ground floor and three bedrooms on the first floor, gross rental, £32 a year; superficial area, 2,311 8-9 square yards. Mr John Nevins, of Gladstone-street was the purchaser, the price being £570. A freehold plot of building land, with extensive southerly frontage of 128 yards or thereabouts to the high road, together with the two cottages, out- houses, and conveniences thereto, comprised Lot 5. Gross rent of cottages, £l5 12s a year ; superficial area, 6,349 5-9 square yards. Mrs Ann Daintith,of the Black Horse Inn, Culcheth, became the purchaser, the lot being knocked down to her for £3lO. The last lot consisted of a freehold field, having a front- age to Dam-lane. Rent, £2O a year; superficial area ..£34,979 square yards. Bidding commenced at £4OO and Mrs Alice Hepherl, of Woolston, secured the lot for £485. Messrs Jenkins and Company, of Bank Chambers, were tin solicitors to the vendor.

Messages from South Africa.

Ministers to Meet ro-Day.

LONDON, THURSDAY NIGHT.

A meeting of Cabinet Ministers has been sum- moned for to-morrow, and it is understood that the Council is the direct outcome uf South African news.

"Peace is Practically Assured."

The " Daily Chronicle "says :—" We are enabled to state on excellent authority that as the out- come of the conference at Pretoria between the Boer leaders and Lords Milner and Kitchener peace is practically assured. The most sanguine hopes—based on communications received from South Africa—find expression in official circles. Unle.s some unlooked for hitch should occur at the eleventh hour—and this, in view of the very advanced stage that has been reached in the negotiations, is regarded as improbable—an almost immediate cessation of hostilities resulting from the Boer acceptance of the British terms is antici- pated: Indeed a message from Lord Kitchener con- veying the welcome intelligence that the long and costly struggle is at an end is expected any moment

ST HELENS.

SUDDEN DEATH OF A ST, HELENS CONTRACTOR.

Mr Peter Tickle, one of the best-known builders and contractors in St. Helens, died very suddenly about three o'clock on Saturday afternoon. Mr Tickle was at business as usual in the morning, but was suddenly seized with:he_rt failure, and died before Dr Jackson could get to his residence; He was fifty-nine years of age. Besides his business as a builder and contractor,he bad successfully carried on the Rainford Brick Works, near Fleet-lane,Parr. One of his notable contracts was the rebuilding of the St. Helens Theatre, which was carried out over a year ago. Be leaves a widow and seven children. Mr. Tickle was busily engaged in the town up to about half-past two on Saturday afternoon, and then started to walk borne briskly. On arriving at his residence' Park Mount, be sat down on the coucn, and said that he was tired. His daughter asked to be allowed to help him off with his coat, and he stood up for this purpose, but before one sleeve had been pulled off he sank down and died. Deceased was elected a sidesman of St. Mark's Church last Easter, and on Sun- day morning the vicar made a sympathetic reference. STI HELENS RECREATION FOOTBALL CLUB. The annual meeting of the members of the St Helens Recreation Football Club was held on Saturday evening at Messrs Pilkington's glassworks reading-roorn. Mr Bowles presided over an attendance of about 150. The secretary (Mr Joseph Wood) gave a favourable account lof the doings of the club in the Lancashire Combination matches. The team had perhaps not been quite so con- sistent as usual, bat several of the big teams, notably Everton Reserve, bad been disposed of in brilliant , fashion. The accounts were, as usual, merged in the general balance-sheet for the Recreation clubs, but not withstanding increased opposition, the gate receipts bad been slightly in excess of the previous season. All the officers were re-elected with the exception that Mr R Sidler retired from the post ca assistant secretary, and Mr G Butcher was appointed assistant to Mr: Joseph Wood, the secretary. Th committee was re-elected, with Mr Alcock, from the flattening department,in place of Mr Butcher. It was proposed and carried that at all future annual meetings a balance-sheet showing the working of the football club alone should be prepared for submission to the annual meeting. THE SCHOLARS ANNUAL FIELD DAY.

Fortunately Whit Monday morning was nee and bright, and the school children starte.4 out to the various schools in the hope that their.annual field-day was going to be a success. The procession, organised ny the Rev. Father Hearne, priest of the Sacred Heart, attracted very great attention. Accompanied by four brass bands, the children and membors of the guilds paraded the principal streets, carrying a very handsome set of banners. The banners were of distinc- tive colours, and the children accompanying I each were dressed en suite, and the various groups presmted a most charming picture. The 2,000 children attending the Parish Church schools, along with the Men and Women's Bible classes, had their annual field-day and sports as Denton's-green. Mr. Joseph Robinson, by whose kindness the ground was placed at the disposal of the Bible class and ashools,performed the opening ceremony. The Bible class sports attracted considerable attention. THE WHITSUNTIDE HOLIDAY, Railway Traffic from Warrington.

The opportunities afforded to the working class population of Warrington of spending a day or two away from their everyday surroundings are so few in number, that an occasion like the Whitsuntide holiday is generally taken advantage of to visit seaside and other resorts. This year, however, the climati.l conditions during the week-end were so unsettled that many people who would otherwise have availed themselves of the special facilities for " seeking fresh fields and pastures new " decided to remain at home. Despite this fact, however,there was a considerable exodus from the town, as is shown by the:railwa3 companies' excursion traffic returns. A violent thunderstorm visited the district on Saturday afternoon, and occasioned gloomy fore- bodings as to the prospects for the two following days. On Sunday the westher was showery and a rather cold wind rendered the condi- tions the reverse to the realization of the average Idea of holiday weather. The elements were in a fickle mood on Monday. In the morning there were several r showers, accompanied by a strong wind, but notwithstanding this, many people left the town for various resorts. During the afternoon and evening the rain kept off, but the sky was continually over- clouded. There were frequent spells of brilliant sunshine, however, but the cold wind continued throughout the day. Large numbers of the inhabi- tants who remained in town indulged in rides on the tram cars, end the scene at Rylands-street was an animated one. Each car as it came up, both to Latchford and Sankey Bridges, was rapidly filled, many riding there and back without leaving their seats. _ _

===== 0002601_19020523_art0136.txt | included=true | included =====
Excursion to Llangollen.

A most enjoyable excursion to Llaugollen organized by Mr. Arthur Ed waras, Bank Quay, took place on Monday. The ,arty numbering 42 of the residents of the district, travelled by saloon to Llangollen, coffep, biscuits and cigars being provided ea route by Mr Edwards. On arrival at their destination convey- ances were in waiting to c905e7 the party to the Aqueduct, and from thence to Chirk. The scenery in the district was much admired. The excur- sionists were then driven to the Waverley Hotel, where an excellent dinner and tea were provided. The evening was spent in a convival manner, several membera of the COMDIOW contributing songs, &c. A start was made at 8.25 for Warrington, which was reached at about half-past ton. On the return journey sweets were provided by Mr Buchanan. The presen- tation will be made on Thursday evening next at the Wellington Hotel. MAY QUEEN FESTIVAL AT EARLESTOWN. A revival of this festival will be welcomed in Earlestown. From an advertisement it will be seen that a festival will take place on Saturday, the 31st inst., the Market ground being enclosed for the pur- pose. The proceeds are to be handed over to the fund being raised for the completion of St. John's Church. and given a fine day, there should be not only a good spectacular display, but a substantial addition to the funds. GOOD TEMPLARY. The weekly meeting of the 1.0.G.T. was held in the Wargrave Hall on Monday. Bro J W Hopkins GSJT paper "The possibilities of Good Templary " was read in sections by Bros Woods, Clarke, Cutler, and J Warburton after which an interesting discus- sion followed. The paper was interspersed by suit- able odes, making a very impressive service. A GOLEORNE SEPTUAGENARIAN BURNED TO DEATH. The death took place at Leigh Union Infirmary on Saturday night of John Lowe, aged seventy-three, a widower, of Golborue Dale, Golborne, who was ad- mitted to that institution on Friday suffering from severe burns to the arms, back, and leg. Deceased was standing undressed before the fire when his shirt got ablaze, and before the flames could be extinguished he was shockingly burnt, being in a dying condition when taken to the infirmary. WHITSUNTIDE AT EA.RLESTOWN• Fortunately Monday was fine, but the Whitson holidays are not generally observed at Earlestown. All the tradespeople closed their places of business for the day ;''and the workmen at the works of Messrs Siete and Co, Messrs T and T Vicars, and Messrs McCorquodale and Co had a holiday. A large number of the men at the Vulcan Foundry absented themselves, but they have been penalised in conse- quence. A good number journeyed to Wigan to the Gala at Mesnes Park, but the majority stayed at home to hang about the streets. CHARITY CONCERT AT EARL EsTOWN. On Saturday evening the Town Hall at Earles• town was well filled with a sympathetic audience in connection with the fund being raised for tee Ibenefit of Mr William Jackson, who was seriously injured by being run over by one of Messrs Evans and Co's engines on their private line, a few months ago. An excellent concert was given,the programme being sufficiently varied to suit the taste of the mixed audience which was present. The various items were efficiently eubmitted by the following artistes Miss N Beatty and Miss Griffiths, Messrs W H Male, ll:Humphreys, and John Isherwood. A cinematograph entertainment was given by Pro- fessor J Alexander. Master C Higham efficiently accompanied at the piano. POLICE CASES. At the Newton Police buildings on Monday morn- ing before Colonel Wood, James Chisnall of Hay- dock, was charged with being drunk at Earlestown on Saturday and was fined 2s 6d and costs William Leigh, Newton Common for being drunk in Newton on Sunday was fined 53 and costs,—W Holden, James Holden and Thomas Howarth, Newton Common, for a like offence were fined 2s 6d and costs.—Edward .Aurphy of Widnes who was , charged with being drunk was handed over to the Liverpool police on another charge—EL Gidman a tramp charged with being on enclosed premises was remanded to Warrington. • STRANGE CONDUCT AT NEWTON.—SEART SENTENCE ON AN OLD OFFENDER. At the Warrington County Petty Sessions, on , Wednesday, Henry Gidman, of no settled abode, I was charged with being found on enclosod pre- mises at Newton, on May 18th.—Frederick Jenkin- son, porter, at Newton station, said he resided in Parkside-lane, and on Sunday evening he went into the backyard and found the prisoner sitting in the closet. He ordered him out, and followed him into the road. Prisoner turned back again, and witness ordered him away. Prisoner afterwards went into the backyard of a neighbour's house, and when witness went to him he pulled some instrument out of his pocket as though he would strike him. Wit- ness gave information to the police.—PC. Beneon said he found the prisoner hiding behind a tree. He had a file and several table knives in his posses- sion.—Sergeant Banks said the prisoner told him that morning that had Jenkinson caught hold of him be shouldl have struck him with the file. Prisoner added that "he bad alwaya lived well,and intended to do so. Flu 'would do anything but com mit marder."—Prisonsr made his 24th appe,ararce nnd was committed to gaol for three months with hard labour. . THEFT FROM Atli EARLESTOWN PAWNSHOP. At the Warrington County Petty Sessions on Wednesday, Elizabeth Howarth, of Buttonwood, was:charged with stealing a man's cloth vest, of the value of 48 6cl, from the shop of Hugh Ellis Pritch- ard, pawnbroker, 31, Market-street, Earlessown, on the 2nd of May.—The prosecutor said he saw the vest safe at the doorway of the shop at about six o'clock, and he missed it at half past nine On the Bth lust it was brought to the shop by a woman, who offered it in pledge. He took possession of it and gave information to the police.—A girl in the service of the defendant said that on the Bth of May her mother asked her to get a shilling's worth of groceries. She asked Mrs Howarth for the money as her wage;. Mrs Howarth had not got the money and told witness to take a vest out of a certain drawer and take it to her mother to pawn, so as to get the money. She must get the vest out of pawn again on Friday. Witness took the vest mentioned in the charge from a drawer downstairs, but it now appeared that the vest she should have taken was Mr Howarth's, which was in another drawer —Defendant said she never told tea girl to take a vest to pawn.—P.O. Hughes said he charged the woman with the theft, and she said, "Yes, sir ; I was drunk."—Defend- ant was ordered to pay the costs and was bound over to come up when called upon,

The Lightning Express. Statements which have recently appeared in the Press as to the progress and intentions of the pro- moters of the Manchester-Liverpool electric rail- way to make an immediate start at these places, and Warrington are, it is officially stated, unauthorised and incorrect, and have caused the promoters much annoyance. A meeting of the Board is to be held at an early date to consider what steps next to take, now that the Bill has become an Act, and till such meeting is helrl any further announcement would be premature. If the Liverpool-Manchester mono-rail line shows good working results, says a news agency, the Belgium Government proposes to adopt the same system for the new quick transit scheme between Antwerp and Brussels, The Government seems disposed to place the contract for the proposed line in the hands of a British firm. It is understood that the contractr must deposit :C400,000 and agree to complete the work within one year after taking possession of the Land along the proposed route,

At the Local government Board enquiry held on Wednesday morning at the Town Hall, no rate- ' payers, outside the Council, were present, although they had a perfect right to attend if they had de— sired to do so. We are supposed to have a Property Owners' Association, but for all the influence it exerts on public matters, it might as well be non- ' existent Application was made at the enquiry, amongst other things,for borrowing powers in con- nection with the gas works, and if the proposed alterations will make any improvement in the quality of the gas at present supplied, no one will grumble. On Saturday night the town was in semi- darkness, owing to the wretched illuminant dis- persed from the gas works, and there is certainly plenty of room for improvement

Special emphasis was placed at the enquiry upon the need for a fire engine, but ii was omitted to be stated that if we ,get the engine we have no force of water to render it of any service, Still we are going to build a water tower, but goo:II:lets only knows how they are going to get water to it We have only a couple of bore holes at present, and the pumps take periodical fits of getting out of order. Certainly they are proceeding with the sinking of the wells, and perhaps when these are finished we may get rid of some of our difficulties. At any rate we ars living in hope.

ST GEORGE'S MISSION V VOLUNTEER SERGE&NTS Teams representing the above met in the return fixture at St George's Mission on Wednesday. The home team were in fine form winning by 73 points. South Lancashire Tramways. Excellent progress is being made by the contrac- tors for considerable lengths of the South Ladca- shire tramways. The Tyldesley lines have been completed. and at Leigh the track has been laid from the Lowton boundary to near the junction of the borough with Atherton, and will shortly join up with the length from Hulton. A number of cars have arrived, and will, it is stated, be run on Coronation Day, being at first propelled by horse traction. The tramways will eventually ba worked and lit by electricity, and this week a start has been made at Hulton with the erection of the trol- ley poles, the contract for which has been let to Messrs Blackwell and Co., of London. Considerable headway has been made with the erection of a large generating station at Howe Bridge.
