THE CORONATION.

Sankey Ratepayers and the Festivities.

Mr. Snoddy and His Rights.

The forthcoming Coronation festivities were under consideration at a special parish meeting which was held in the Board Schools, Great Saukey,on Tuesday evening. The chair was occupied by Mr George Thompson, but there was only a small attendance. At the outsetdar J SErlitn (clerk of the Parish Council) read a circular letter from die Local G ,vernmont B lard sanctioning any reasonable expenses woion might be incurred by various lonel authorities, including Parisn Councils, for the purpose of celebrating the Coronado n. Tine Chairman said the matter was discussed at the ilast meeting or the Parish C 31.111011, but it was thought [advisable to call a meeting of the ratepayers of the town- ship to coefer about it. The Council therefore refrained from making any suggestions whatever. He regretted the small attendance, but he hoped those present would determine to make the festivities worthy of Great San- key. Personally, and he believed he was speaking for the Parish Council as a whole, he objected to the levying of a rate on the tewnship. In answer to Mr W D Jeans, sea, The Clerk said that a rate of a penny in the £ would produce £36 153, or possibly £37. Mr Halton said that since the last meeting he had ascertained that in the districts surrounding Warring- ton, it had been suggeetcd In the majority of cases, tha a special rate shod' be levied for Coronalon festivities Since the formation of Great Sankey Parish Counci they had not incurred one penny in special expenses, except for electioneering purposes, and ho felt that on such a memorable oceasien as the Coronation they would not be doing wrong in levying a penny in the £ as a special parish rate. If that were done the two railway companies and the two or three large works in the district would have to contribute in proportion to their ratable value. It was certain that the railway companies would not contribute one farthing unless they were compelled to do so by the levying of the rate, and he thought it would be a shame that they should not do so as they would reap considerable benefit from passenger traffic at Coronation time. Outside subscrip- tions could then be solicited from the landlords and others, and he felt sure that a good sum would be realized. He moved—" That this Parish Meeting hereby resolves that a sum of £36 be applied in aid of a fund for the celebration of Mel Coronation of his Majesty King Edward VII out of the expenses to be raised by the Parish Council for that purpose." Mr Jeans seconded and said he thought the trifling in- crease in the rates would effect the pockets of the people to such a small extent that it was not worth considera- tion. It would get at the pockets of the people in pro- portion to their ratability and means. Mr Snoddy oppoeed the resolution. he did not think the present was an occasion to put their hands into the ratepayers'pockets and compel them to contribute to the fund. He considered it was an opportunity for the loyal and prosperous to come forward and give some evidence of their generosity. He proposed an amend- ment that a subscription list be opened. Mr Jeans : I suggest that Mr Snoddy heads the list. _ _

Mr Snoddv : I .a-rn not a resident. Mr Balton : I am sorry to hear Mr Shoddy's remarks. I thought we were all ratepayers here.— The Clerk : Mr Snoddy is not a parochial el3ctor. Mr Snoddy : lam a ratepayer and shall take all the privileges and advantages of a ratepayer. The Clerk . This as a parish meeting at which only parochial electors can vote. I do not say it would not be in order to allow Mr Suoddy to Blake any observa- tions.

Mr Snoddy It is a public meeting and we are at liberty to express our opinions. Mr Snoddy proceeded to say that his remarks were in harmony with what had been decided in several other distriers.

Mr Halton : Mr Snoddy has spoken before. We want to get on with the business. lam sorry to isee Mr Seoddy causing an obstruction here.

Mr Soddy (co the Chairman) : Will you allow me the liberty to express my opinion ? rhe Chairman : Lt Mr Snoddy have a word or two if he wants. He has a right to let us know wnat he is going to speak about..

Mr Sooddy : My view is adopted by at least two parishes in the neighbourhood, Scockten Heath and Pen- keth, who are meeting the expenses by open subscrip- tion. I contend that I am at perfect liberty to express my views, or else, why call a public meeting ? lam surprised that Mr Halton, who is a public man, should attack me as be has done to-night. Mr Halton : lam awfully sorry if I have hurt Mr Snoddy's feelings, and I shall at once apologise, but I think we are giving him a long hearing. We want to get on with the business.

Mr Suoddy : I have never been el much interrupted and interfered with in my life before. The Cnairman : I am advised that Mr Sooddy is quite in order in moving the amendment. I ask if any one will second it.

As there was no seconder, tbe amendment fell to the ground. . ..

Mr Hodgkinson spoke in support of the resolution. He did not see where they %conic' get the money from if they excluded the railway companies and works. They ought to do honour to an occasion like that, and it was not worth while falling out about a penny in the Z. If there was a large number ot wealthy people in the:district to subscribe to the fund, he should object to the levying of the rate. He objected to levying a rate, oa principle, but he did not think they could get the money in Sankey any other way. Mr Suoddy said his objection was to the principle. He was not strongly opposed to the rate but he preferred the other way of raising the money. The motion was curried unanimously, Mr Snoddy, in accordance with the Clerk's ruling, refraining from voting. On the motion of Mr Jeans, seconded by Mr-Halton, it was decided that a subscription list be opened to supplement the amoucit raised by the rate. _

Mr Jeans pioposed that a co-rnanittee --ho appointed to carry out the arrangements, consisting of all the gentlemen present. Ho did not exclude any who were not ratepayers, and he suggested that Mr Snoddy's name b 3 put on tho committee as well as that of Mr Hodgkinson.

Mr- Snoddy de .11ined to serve, as ho wax already en- gaged in connection with the Ponketi celebration, but eventually a general committee was appointed consist- ing of all present, with the exception named. Considerable discussion ensued with regard to the treat to the children it being stated that the Church people had decided to hold the umal Walking Day treat this year.. _

It was decided that a sub-committee be appointed consisting of the Chairman and Mesars Halton, Jeans, Hodgkinson, and Taylor to arrange the details. It was also resolved that the festivities take place on Coronation Day,

The Arrangements at Burtonvrood.

A committee, t7.onsisting of the Parish Council, tile School Board, their respective clerks and the managers of the Voluntary Schools, have held two meetings, and have decided that the celebration be held on June 26th, as follows procession of school children ; all the children attending the public elementary schools of the district to be given a tea, and east' to be presented with a cup bearing the portrait of the King and Queen; all the inhabitants of Burtonwood over 60 to be given a tea on the same day, the remainder of the day to be devoted t 3 sports fier the children with a display of fire balloons. The expenses will be defrayed out of the rates. We understand that the cast will amount to about £4O. In addition to the above, the Clerk to the School Bsard (Mr. W Webster) will present each child attending the public elementary schools of the district with a commemorative medal.

Halton Festivities.

On Saturday a meeting of the Halton Relief Com- mittee was held, under the presidency of llar R Hughes. at the request of the Halton Parochial Committee+, to consider the request of the Halton Coronation Committee to graat from their funds a sum of money to provide a supper for aged people over 60 years of age, in connec- tion with the coronation expenses. Mr T Wright said that he had been told by a friend who was a lawyer that it would not be legal to spend the money as proposed, unless the donors gave their con- sent.—The Rev G D Wray said that when be received the letter, asking him to summon the committee he felt certain that it would net be legal, and wrote to the two chief donors, to ascertain their views. He read letters from Sir Richard M Brooke, Bart., and Sir John T , Brunner, Bart., both of whom were opposed to the 'money being spent on Coronation festivities. He also I read letters from two members of the Relief Committee I who were unable to be present at the meeting, Mr H Reynold (treasurer) and Mr J Stokes also disapproving ' of this manner of speeding the money. Mr Wray expressed his opinion that the relief fund was a charity, and that it would be a very , daegerous precedent to appropriate the money for any other purpose than that for which' it was originally given. Considering all that was said at theitime of the passing of the Parish Councils Billond the election of the.fir.st Cot.,neil, with regard to the sup- posed tnisapprepriatien of charitable funds, they ought to be very careful what they did in this matter. He regarded the committee, not only as appointed to dis- tribute the fund, but also as trustees of the fund, and if they spent the money in eating and drinking now, a time would come when the money would be wanted,and then they would b 3 accused of having misappropriatPd a charitable fund for a feast. Instead of regretting that the balance of £37 12s was lying idle, they ought to he very thankful that no occasion bad arisen during the past seven years for spending it. An occasion might easily arise, perhaps quite unforeseen, when they would be glad that they had the money in the bank for the purpose for which it was originally intended.—After Considerable diseussien it was • ventually resolved that the relief fund was not available for Coronation festivi- ties.

The Bonfires Scheme.

Meeting of the Central Committee.

TO THE EDITOR.

Dear sir,—We rely so much upon the co•operation of the Press in making the Coronation Bonfires movement a success, tha; we venture to send you a report of the proceedings of the committee called together last week 3 under the ;presidency of our chairman—Viscount Cran- borne. We adjoin a ii3t at the bonfires in the various caunties, up to date, a which we have had notification. We would specially urge that the Bot.tire Committee 6hould not be discouraged from joining the movement, because they happen to hays no commanding eminence on which to build their bonfires. The pleasure and loyalty round a bonfire on June 26th will not be dimin- ished because their bonfire will not be far seen. At the sante time we ask that Bonfire Committees shall take every precaution against damage to the surroundings of their.bonfires,prevent any firing:ef furze or heather,and discourage any irresponsible and indiscriminate bon- fires which might lead to loss or harm to property. We would also ask that those who build them should, if possible, have photographs taken of the bonfire masses, both before and after lighting them, and that copies may be sent to the secretaries for a permanent record. We wish it to be known that ample instructions will be forwarded by Messrs Pain, the Pyrotechionists, with each rocket and that, with the simplest care, no acci- dents need be feared in their discharge. We have the honour to remain :

===== 0002601_19020523_art0143.txt | included=true | included =====
A. G. BOSCAWEN, M.P., J. M.CALMONT, M.P., H. M. CADELL, G. IL MIL WARD, H. D. RAWNSLEY, Hon. Sacs.

At a well attended meeting of the Committee on Tuesday last, Viscount Cranborne in the chair, the Hon Secretaries reported that from all parts of Great Britain and Ireland there was evidence of enthusiasm for the bonfire movement. Canon Rawnsley attributed part of the enthusiasm to the fact that it bad become known that His Majesty was interested in and pleased to hear of the progress of the movement, and part to the memory of the pleasure given to a great number of people by the bonfires of the Jubilee and Diamond Jubilee. He urged upon all present. through the local press and through their Lords Lieuten- ant and Chairmen of the County Councils, to invite their various counties to emulation in the matter of the bonfire display on June 26th, and trusted that those who had the building of the bonfires in charge, would take care that some permanent record should be kept of them by photography, and copies be sent to the don. Secre- taries. As to rockets he reported that the detonating ships rocket (8 oz.), electric floating signal rocket (24 oz.), and magnesium shower star rocket (12 oz.), supplied by Messrs Pain and Soo, dummy specimens of which were on view in the room, were found on trial to be a success, and that their coloured star rockets (8 oz.) were effective. He urged that those who used coloured fire sheald remember that it was best to pour some of the powder from the tins into flower-pot saucers, and distribute these at points twenty yards apart, if the best effect possible in lighting up the boafire was to be obtained.

Mr Boscawen, M.P., stated that the proprietors of the Great Wheel had signified their willingness to co-operate with the committee and give the rocket signal to London and neighbourhood at 955 from the tap of the Great Wheel, and illuminate the wheel on June 26. He also reported that a very large number of bonfires bad been arranged for in the counties south of the Thames.

He was also able to report from Colonel M'Calmont, who could not be present. that news of bonfires had been received from all parts of Ireland. Mr H M Cadet' gave an account of the building of the Bonnytown Bonfire at the Diamond Jubilee, and urged strongly that sheer-legs or stout poles connected at the head and set up in tripod fashion, should be used as central scaffolding and for stability to the bonfire masses. He spoke of the use of cords ze and lateral supports to prevent accident from windstorm on the heights, when the mass was built, and the need of vigil- ance to prevent mischievous setting on firs of the mass before the appointed hour, as happened in one or two places on a previous occasion. He undertook the hon. secretary's work for Scotland.

" Sleepy " grass is found in New Mexico, Texas and Siberia. It has a most injurious f ffsct on horses and sheep, being a strong narcotic or sedative and causing profound sleep or stupor lestink 24 Co 43 hours. A horse after eating it is a pitiable object, its head and tail drooping, its body quivering and perspiration pouring down its sides. • A remarkable innovation i 9 about to be made in Paris. The policemen on night duty are to have electric lights on various parts of their uniforms and aithe extremities of their batons. Those will be worked by pressing a waistcoat button, and los used in the regulation of the street traffic. Tonacco SMOKE AVERTS DISEASE.—Added no 71 to the joys of tobacco is the knowledge that smoking may pre- vent some diseases. Dr Damon has studied the action of tobacco smoke upon the various organisms found in the cavity of the mouth and has found that while it has no effect upon typhoid fever germs or tetanus (lockjaw) It greatly retards the growth of the bacilli of influenza, I of diphtheria, and of consumption.
